#2892. WNBA Finals MVP Gabby Williams: 'Our existence is political'

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

Golden State Valkyries star Gabby Williams doesn't expect the political and cultural issues swirling around the WNBA to go away anytime soon.

Fresh off the Valkyries' first championship and winning the Finals MVP award, Williams spoke with CNBC's Alex Sherman about the league's place in those conversations. The WNBA has been at the forefront of several political and cultural issues this season, ranging from race to sexuality.

"I think as a female athlete, just our existence is political," Williams said. "Being a female athlete just automatically becomes politicized because we're in a space where maybe we weren't welcome before."

Williams said that she and many of her fellow athletes understand the fact that that playing professional basketball has brought them a larger platform.

"I think it's something that we embrace as athletes," Williams said, pointing to players' past involvement in political and social causes, including the 2020 Georgia Senate runoff and the Black Lives Matter movement. "I think that'll continue," she said.

The 30-year-old basketball player, born in Nevada to an American father and a French mother, said she grew up watching the Black Lives Matter movement while a student-athlete at the University of Connecticut.

"Realizing, oh, wow, these aren't just basketball players. They're leaders in our community. I was actually looking forward to that part of the W," she said.

Williams has used her platform to speak out in support of transgender rights, a topic that became a flashpoint for the league this season after Indiana Fever player Sophie Cunningham argued that transgender women should not compete in women's sports.

Williams has maintained that sports should be open to everyone, and she's revealed that's she gotten hateful messages and death threats because of her views.

Williams said the Valkyries’ home arena, the Chase Center, which has become known as "Ballhalla" for its electric atmosphere, helped her feel welcome and gave her the confidence to thrive this season.

"Being in Ballhalla allowed me to be myself," Williams said. "As soon as I got there, I felt so welcomed. In the preseason game, I didn't even play, and people were like screaming for me and welcoming me. And it just gives me a confidence, that I think I can only get from Ballhalla."

#2891. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2890. Mack Hollins happy with Patriots’ response after post-game challenge

The New England Patriots were, unsurprisingly, not happy with the performance they put forward last Sunday against the Green Bay Packers.

No player verbalized that more clearly than wide receiver Mack Hollins, who put a challenge to his teammates.

"If you're not ready to come to work Tuesday and Wednesday and get on that plane and work all this week, you're probably not meant for this team," he said.

"If there are guys on this team that are OK with that, I'm sure there are 31 other teams they can go to and maybe they can live like that. But we're trying to get stuff done about this week."

So, with the smoke having settled on the 45-27 defeat, how does Hollins feel about the state of the Patriots? It appears better. Talking to the media on Wednesday, before the team's flight to Germany for its upcoming international game with the Detroit Lions, he added further context to his prior comments and along the way praised the team's response to its fourth loss of the season.

"I think it's good," the 33-year-old said. "We're excited for another opportunity, and I think the guys are focused on the 'one week at a time' mentality, which is a good thing. You're trying to reach too far ahead or too far behind you, it's hard to know where your feet are."

Hollins was not the only member of the organization to highlight the Patriots' work ethic and mental toughness this week. Head coach Mike Vrabel did as well, effectively echoing what the wideout said on Sunday.

And even with some time having passed, Hollins stood by every word he said.

"If you come after that game and said, 'We did enough, it's not my fault,' you're not able to look in the mirror and say, 'I can get better.' It's more of that than guys aren't working or guys aren't doing things," he explained.

"If you get this result and you're OK with it, and you're like, 'I don't need to come to practice next week and come to work, and I'd rather be somewhere where I can just relax and not do anything,' this isn't the place for you. I always stand by that, because I don't want to play with guys that are like that. I don't think we have guys like that, because, like we've said, we all we are, we all we need. The guys that are here, they come to play. Is the result always want we want? Hell, no. But do you continue to push that and get better? Yes."

#2888. Social Security could pay full benefits until 2059 if taxable payroll kept pace with GDP: AARP report

Social Security's taxable payroll, which provides most of the revenue for the program that pays monthly benefits to millions of Americans, has fallen as a share of gross domestic product over the past four decades, according to a new report from the AARP Public Policy Institute.

Had payroll tax revenue kept up with GDP, the program could pay full benefits until 2059, according to the research — an additional 25 years beyond the latest projected depletion date of 2034 for the program's combined funds. The AARP Public Policy Institute provides policy research, analysis and development at AARP, a nonprofit, nonpartisan organization that represents Americans ages 50 and over.

"Social Security's finances have weakened not simply because America has grown older, but because the economy has changed, while the program's revenue system has not," Richard Johnson, vice president of financial security at the AARP Public Policy Institute, said during a Tuesday press briefing on the research.

"America's economy is larger and more productive than it was four decades ago, but Social Security is receiving support from a smaller share of that economy," Johnson said.

Social Security's trustees projected in June that the program's combined retirement and disability trust funds may be depleted in the third quarter of 2034. Unless Congress takes action, 83% of scheduled benefits will be payable at that time, according to the trustees' projections.

The trustees' report also included an earlier projected depletion date for Social Security's retirement trust fund — the fourth quarter of 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable. However, the AARP said it based its analysis on the later 2034 date because Congress has shifted money between the funds in the past.

The program's current shortfall is worse than lawmakers had anticipated in 1983, when the last major Social Security reforms were enacted.

Rising income inequality means the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings — a percentage lawmakers expected to remain constant when they made the 1983 reforms, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized there, according to the institute.

AARP points to an aging population as another reason for Social Security's funding challenges. The worker-to-beneficiary ratio will fall from 3.4 to 2.4 between 2000 and 2030. Falling fertility rates are also shrinking the worker-to-beneficiary ratio.

Shifting income patterns have also meant that a smaller share of U.S. GDP is subject to the Social Security payroll tax. Taxable payroll has fallen from 41% of GDP in 1983 to 34% as of 2025, a level Social Security's trustees project will remain, according to the AARP.

AARP attributes the decline to a shift from wages toward business and investment income, which generally isn't subject to payroll taxes. Automation has reduced demand for workers, and pass-through businesses allow some owners to report compensation as profits rather than wages, according to the report.

Social Security would have collected $10.8 trillion in additional funding between 1984 and 2034 if taxable payroll had stayed at 41% of GDP, according to the AARP. That figure is in inflation-adjusted 2025 dollars.

In 2026, up to $184,500 in wages are subject to the Social Security payroll tax.

Payroll taxes accounted for 91% of Social Security's revenues in 2025, according to AARP's research, while 5% came from interest income and 4% was through federal income taxes paid by high-income beneficiaries on their benefits.

To increase Social Security's revenues, lawmakers may consider changes that make high-income taxpayers contribute more to the program while sparing low- to moderate-income taxpayers, according to the AARP report.

"The fix for Social Security isn't cutting earned benefits; it's rebuilding the revenue behind them," Johnson said.

Raising the payroll tax threshold — which is incrementally adjusted each year to keep up with average wages — would not affect most workers, since just 6% of individuals had earnings at or above the cap in 2024, according to the AARP.

In 2027, the Social Security taxable maximum is set to increase to $192,300.

Bills have been introduced in Congress to raise the cap to apply to higher thresholds, such as $250,000 or $400,000, or to eliminate it altogether. Getting rid of the cap would close 67% of the 75-year funding gap if workers do not receive a benefit increase in exchange for those contributions, according to AARP research based on the Social Security Administration's assessments.

Another option: Lawmakers may also shore up the program by taxing income not currently subject to Social Security payroll taxes, according to the AARP, following an increase in business activity through pass-through entities — such as sole proprietorships, partnerships, limited liability companies and S corporations.

Lawmakers could create a new tax on investment income to help fund Social Security's trust funds. A new 12.4% tax on investment income for active S corp. officers and limited partners would close about 25% of Social Security's 75-year financing gap, according to the AARP, which cited research from Social Security's actuaries.

One option not mentioned in the AARP research is raising the payroll tax rate, which is currently 6.2% for workers and employers, or 12.4% combined. The Social Security trustees estimated that an increase in the combined rate to 16.65% beginning in January 2026 would have made the combined program solvent for the 75-year period ending in 2100.

A national survey conducted in February found 62% of individuals would support gradually increasing the payroll tax from 6.2% to 6.5%, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. That poll included 2,509 adults.

The 1983 Social Security reforms included changes to benefits, such as gradually raising the retirement age from 65 to 67, delaying the cost-of-living adjustment by six months and taxing Social Security benefits.

Research shows those changes have reduced lifetime retirement benefits by about 19% for people turning 65 in 2026 compared to what they would have received under the previous rules, Jenn Jones, vice president of financial security and livable communities at the AARP, said during the Tuesday press briefing.

While the AARP has not endorsed any specific benefit changes, Jones said the organization opposes cuts to monthly checks.

"Millions of Americans — we hear from them every day — cannot afford to lose a penny," Jones said.

#2887. Jim Cramer says investors need to pay attention to CoreWeave. Here's why

CNBC's Jim Cramer said Wednesday that he's becoming less skeptical of CoreWeave after the neocloud's strong earnings and the CEO's "refreshing and reassuring" comments.

CoreWeave shares surged nearly 25% on Wednesday after the company reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and raised its 2026 guidance. The results, which were out Tuesday evening, helped ease concerns about a slowdown in AI infrastructure spending. CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator's subsequent interview with Cramer on CNBC just after Wall Street's opening bell offered further reasons for optimism.

One of the most encouraging takeaways from Intrator was disclosure that demand for CoreWeave's computing capacity is 10 times greater than what the company can supply. It was also discussed on the post-earnings conference call. That dynamic has allowed CoreWeave to consistently increase prices, supporting stronger gross margins even as it spends heavily to expand its infrastructure.

Intrator also addressed concerns about CoreWeave's substantial debt load, which has weighed heavily on its stock. Despite Wednesday's surge, shares are still down 15% year to date and down 55% from its 52-week high of $138 on May 6. The past month has been particularly tumultuous for the neoclouds, which also include Iren and Nebius. Iren reported strong earnings last week. Nebius reports its quarter Thursday morning.

Rather than relying solely on traditional bond markets, CoreWeave has increasingly turned to financing backed by its equipment and customer contracts. "When I pressed him, asking why he doesn't just go into the open market and buy these bonds that are trading so poorly, he admitted that he's been tempted to, but ultimately he still believes he can make an even better return building data centers," Cramer said. "Take that red flag off the table."

Another surprising revelation involved the value of CoreWeave's Nvidia chips. Intrator said some H100 chips purchased four years ago are now being contracted at premiums of 20% to 50% above its original cost, suggesting the equipment is retaining its economic value longer than many investors anticipated.

The Intrator interview also helped ease concerns about growing political opposition to data center construction. The CEO maintained that demand for computing power would continue to drive expansion, even if stricter regulations pushed some projects overseas.

For Cramer, the comments offered a welcome counterpoint to the recent pessimism surrounding AI infrastructure stocks. While he has grown more cautious about the sector amid political and financing risks, the "Mad Money" host maintained that abandoning it altogether could mean missing substantial upside. "We've taken some data center and related stocks off the table, unfortunately not Nvidia, [for the CNBC Investing Club] because our composure and our prudence dictates that to have too much data center is still too dicey. Still, we've left plenty of exposure to the group, which includes semis, software, networking, builders, turbines and cooling equipment, because we think the AI theme has much more room to run."

The Club does not own any neoclouds. But it does have diversified, albeit recently reduced, exposure to the AI boom via chip stocks and names that help run and power data centers.

#2886. Lazio e Roma, undici gare senza ko: l’Inter guida il differenziale xG

La squadra di Chivu registra un valore di +18,20; i biancocelesti segnano più di quanto producano secondo l’indicatore.

Lazio e Roma sono ancora imbattute dopo undici giornate e, nell’analisi della corsa scudetto, l’Inter appare la rivale più attrezzata per inseguirle. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Messaggero, I nerazzurri uniscono nove vittorie, un pareggio e una sconfitta al miglior differenziale di expected goals del campionato.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Le squadre di Gattuso e Gasperini hanno raggiunto il vertice attraverso percorsi diversi. Milan e Como restano nella corsa, mentre Juventus e Napoli attraversano difficoltà che, al momento, rendono più complicato considerarle antagoniste delle due romane.

Il primato delle due squadre della Capitale non era atteso in questi termini. Gasperini e Gattuso propongono un calcio per alcuni aspetti simile, pur guidando formazioni diverse. Entrambe sono rimaste imbattute nel confronto con le grandi del Nord e con la grande del Sud, trasformandosi in protagoniste della Serie A nel giro di undici giornate.

È la prima volta nella storia della Serie A che due club della stessa città restano imbattuti per undici partite. I loro percorsi differenti hanno prodotto lo stesso risultato in classifica. Alle loro spalle, però, le inseguitrici attendono scontri diretti e possibili passi falsi: anche gli infortuni possono pesare sulla tenuta delle squadre in vetta.

Il quadro comprende la Juventus, lontana dalle attese, e il Napoli, campione d’Italia due volte nelle ultime tre stagioni ma ora a 14 punti dalla capolista. Il Milan è invece a tre lunghezze dalla Roma e a cinque dalla Lazio.

Tra le candidate a contrastare il primato romano, l’Inter emerge per risultati, organico e guida tecnica. La squadra di Chivu ha segnato 28 gol: soltanto la Roma, con 31, ha fatto meglio. Il dato più significativo nell’analisi delle occasioni è però il differenziale di expected goals, che confronta la qualità delle opportunità create con quella delle occasioni concesse.

Secondo i dati di xgEdge, l’Inter guida questa graduatoria con +18,20 xG, circa sei punti più della Roma e un valore quasi cinque volte quello della Lazio. La squadra di Pio Esposito produce dunque occasioni pericolose in misura nettamente superiore a quelle che lascia agli avversari, al di là dei risultati già ottenuti.

La Roma segue a quota +12; vengono poi Como, Milan e Lazio. I biancocelesti rappresentano un caso particolare: segnano più di quanto producano secondo questo indicatore. La squadra di Gattuso sfrutta le opportunità offensive e ne ricava punti.

Il Milan di Amorim resta vicino alle romane, ma deve fare a meno di Gabbia, Tomori e Saelemaekers, tre elementi importanti della rosa, oltre a Chukwueze e Loftus-Cheek. Il Como, costruito per salire in classifica, è l’unica italiana tra le prime otto in Champions League. In campionato, però, ha incontrato difficoltà contro Inter e Frosinone.

Dopo la sconfitta per 2-0, Fabregas ha riconosciuto la superiorità dei nerazzurri. La sua squadra dispone di individualità forti, ma deve ancora accumulare esperienza per competere per lo scudetto. Chivu, invece, attende un passo falso delle romane per tornare capolista.

#2885. The world's super-rich are piling into alternative investments. Here's why

The world's wealthiest investors are increasingly looking beyond stocks and bonds, pouring money into hedge funds, private equity and infrastructure assets, as market volatility and fears about stubborn inflation spur diversification.

From Asian billionaires to global family offices, alternative investments are becoming a bigger part of portfolios once dominated by traditional assets, said industry watchers

JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in Asia-Pacific surged 40% over the past 12 months, according to Dan Watkins, the firm's regional chief executive officer, underscoring the growing appetite among high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth clients.

"So you really have this huge appetite for alternative exposure happening in that ultra-high-net-worth, high-net-worth category," Watkins said recently at a media summit in Singapore.

The shift comes as investors grapple with increasingly concentrated equity markets, geopolitical uncertainty and concerns that stocks and bonds may not provide the same protection against market downturns as they once did, experts said.

"We are seeing a sharp increase in planned asset allocation to alternatives among the family offices we speak to," said John Woods, chief investment officer for Asia at Lombard Odier.

Alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios, surpassing traditional assets for the first time, according to BNY Wealth. Traditional assets accounted for 41% of allocations, the report found.

Wealthy investors are seeking diversification, income and access to investments outside listed markets, and concerns over elevated valuations, tight credit spreads and the growing correlation between stocks and bonds have accelerated the trend, Woods added.

Hedge funds are emerging as a favored option among wealthy investors seeking protection against market swings, particularly as traditional diversification strategies face growing challenges.

Olaolu Aganga, head of portfolio construction and analytics at Citi Wealth, said hedge funds have attracted the strongest recent interest among qualified clients, who are seeking returns less dependent on the direction of equity markets.

Private credit is another area drawing interest, offering investors contractual income streams, while some hedge fund strategies seek to profit from differences in performance between companies rather than relying on broad market rallies, said Woods.

Citi's 2026 survey of 351 family offices found that inflation was their biggest concern, followed by interest rates, financial system stability and market volatility.

Wealthy families are increasingly seeking investments that can generate income and help preserve purchasing power amid persistent inflation and interest-rate uncertainty, while diversifying portfolios against market volatility, according to Aganga.

The Citi survey, however, also showed public equities recorded the strongest net increase in allocations, suggesting wealthy investors are adding alternatives rather than abandoning traditional markets. "This is not a wholesale rotation away from public markets," Woods said.

Infrastructure has emerged as another major beneficiary of the shift, with the artificial intelligence boom creating enormous demand for data centers, electricity networks and digital connectivity.

Thomas Roland Guyot, head of private markets investment specialists for Southeast Asia at UBS Unified Global Alternatives, said investors are increasingly targeting assets that support AI, electrification and energy security.

Such investments potentially offer a combination of long-term growth and relatively predictable cash flows, particularly where revenues are supported by essential demand or long-term contracts.

Investor appetite is already showing up in fund flows. Net inflows into open-ended infrastructure funds rose 30% year on year in the 12 months through the first quarter of 2026, according to PitchBook data cited by UBS.

Private equity remains a cornerstone of wealthy investors' alternative portfolios, although they are becoming more selective. Family offices are increasingly favoring direct investments in companies, co-investments alongside private equity managers and purchases of existing fund stakes on the secondary market, rather than relying solely on traditional pooled funds, according to Citi Wealth's Aganga.

Such approaches can give investors greater control over their investments or opportunities to acquire assets at discounts, said Lombard Odier's Woods.

The broader shift is also being facilitated by the growth of evergreen and semi-liquid funds, which have lowered minimum investment thresholds and reduced some administrative hurdles associated with traditional private-market funds, Woods added.

Investing into alternative assets does not come without risks, even as mitigating those is driving flows.

Private investments can lock up capital for years, while valuations may be slow to reflect deteriorating market conditions. Even funds marketed as semi-liquid can restrict withdrawals when redemption requests surge.

"Improved access does not equal liquidity," JPMorgan's Watkins said.

Citi's Aganga also warned that performance can vary significantly between managers, while private assets may appear less volatile than they actually are because valuations are updated less frequently.

Investors must also guard against unintended concentration. A portfolio holding data centers through real estate, infrastructure and private equity funds may appear diversified while remaining heavily exposed to the same AI investment cycle, UBS' Guyot said.

For now, the flow of capital into alternative assets shows their increasing appeal among the rich, though not at the expense of traditional investment, with Woods pointing out that much of the new investment is being funded by fresh capital and excess cash rather than large-scale sales of stocks and bonds.

#2884. Meta head of infrastructure will leave and be succeeded by former AWS cloud exec

Meta's long-time infrastructure leader will leave the company in the new year, setting the stage for a recently hired former Amazon Web Services executive to oversee its massive data center technical operations.

Santosh Janardhan, Meta's head of infrastructure and co-head of engineering, revealed his upcoming departure on Wednesday in an internal memo obtained by CNBC. Janardhan, who has worked at Meta since 2009, said "it's time to pass the baton" to former AWS senior vice president David Brown, who joined Meta in July from AWS.

The executive move underscores Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg's efforts to expand the company's data center operations under newer leaders at a time of increasing investor scrutiny on capital expenditure spending by the social media giant and its hyperscaler rivals.

Brown will now formally lead Meta's infrastructure efforts as part of the Meta Compute initiative, which is spearheaded by the company's president and vice chairman Dina Powell McCormick and technical leader Daniel Gross. Zuckerberg is relying on these new executives to continue developing cutting-edge data centers and related infrastructure needed to support its AI ambitions.

The company said during its recent third-quarter earnings report that it expects full-year capex to fall in the range of $135 billion to $145 billion. Meta also logged negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion, which S&P Global Ratings noted as part of its decision in late October to revise Meta's credit rating from AA- stable to AA- negative.

Janardhan said in the memo that he will help support Brown through the first quarter of 2027 as part of "the transition."

"As we make the next set of choices about our priorities, this is the right time for Dave to take the lead in shaping that work," Janardhan said in the memo. "Dave has spent his career building and operating dependable infrastructure for a wide range of demanding workloads. He has the instincts, depth and judgment this moment requires."

Bloomberg News first reported on the executive transition.

#2882. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2881. Canada’s ambassador warns Trump tariffs are driving up costs, threatening U.S. investment

Canada's ambassador to the U.S. warned Wednesday that President Donald Trump's tariffs are driving up investment costs and discouraging business expansion, as new Federal Reserve research points to more price hikes for American consumers next year.

"Uncertainty increases your cost of capital," Ambassador Mark Wiseman said at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"If you're going to build a new plant, add a new shift, spend money on R&D, the more certainty you have, the more likely you're going to do that," said Wiseman, a former BlackRock executive who became Canada's ambassador in February.

His warning comes as Washington and Ottawa struggle to resolve a tariff quarrel affecting one of the U.S.'s largest trading relationships. The two countries exchanged roughly $570 billion in goods during the first nine months of 2026, according to U.S. Census Bureau data. That's an increase from the same period in 2025, the first year of Trump's second term, but lower than the comparable nine months during the previous three years, when Joe Biden was president.

U.S. customs duties calculated on imports from Canada reached $10 billion in 2025, compared with $400 million in 2024, according to the Congressional Research Service.

An October study by New York Fed researchers found that nearly 90% of tariff costs were passed on to U.S. importers, with about a quarter of tariff increases eventually reaching consumer goods prices and further price pressures expected in 2027.

The findings come as Labor Department data showed consumer prices rose a higher-than-expected 0.4% in October, adding to inflation concerns after Republicans' sweeping midterm losses.

Trump has defended his tariffs as necessary to protect U.S. manufacturers and workers, accusing Canada of discriminatory trade practices that disadvantage U.S. exporters.

The two governments have resumed negotiations after talks collapsed in August, with discussions focused on reducing tariffs on Canadian steel and automobiles. Trump has imposed duties of up to 50% on certain Canadian products, prompting retaliatory measures targeting billions of dollars in U.S. exports.

Wiseman called for the removal of tariffs imposed under Sections 232 and 338 of U.S. trade law, saying a resolution would help businesses make long-term investment decisions.

The political stakes have shifted following the midterms, with Democrats preparing to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January.

Still, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Tuesday that the Trump administration remains Canada's principal negotiating partner.

"We've had comprehensive free trade with the United States since 1988," Wiseman said, describing Canada's objective as returning "as close as we can to free trade" while creating the certainty businesses need.

European ambassadors echoed those concerns at the B20 summit. Germany's ambassador to the U.S., Jens Hanefeld, said businesses could adapt to tariffs but struggled with an "ever-changing regulatory environment" that forced companies to spend more on lawyers to navigate shifting trade rules.

Speaking on the same panel, France's ambassador, Aurélien Lechevallier, urged greater cooperation on AI and energy rather than continued trade disputes.

"We've spent, I think, a lot of time the last years working and dealing with regulations, tariffs," he said. "Businesses are eager to do more business."

— Correction: This story has been corrected to reflect that Wiseman spoke about adding shifts to plants. A previous version misquoted the ambassador.

#2880. Lutnick should ban Chinese memory maker CXMT from U.S., Sen. Cotton says

Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., is urging the Trump administration to take a harsher stance toward Chinese memory maker ChangXin Memory Technologies.

"CXMT, one of Communist China's national memory chip champions, poses a growing risk to our economic prosperity and military superiority," Cotton wrote in a letter sent Thursday to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick. "The U.S. must take action to prevent CXMT from gaining a stronger foothold in the global market."

CXMT is the world's fourth-largest producer of DRAM  – or dynamic random access memory – which powers many basic functions of computers and smartphones.

Cotton, in a letter first obtained by CNBC, is asking Lutnick to push CXMT onto the department's Bureau of Industry and Security Entity List, which would significantly restrict U.S. firms' ability to do business with the company.

"It basically means that U.S. companies are not allowed to do business with that entity-listed company, and that entity-listed company does not get access to U.S. technology," said Kyle Chan, a Brookings Institution fellow who studies China's technology development.

DRAM is a "commodity," MS Hwang, research director at Counterpoint, told CNBC, meaning it is made to a global standard and is generally interchangeable from one manufacturer to another.

However, with the right production technology, it can also form the building blocks of HBM, or high-bandwidth memory. HBM is an extremely high-demand product that powers AI chips made by Nvidia, Google and Amazon Web Services, which are central to the growth of artificial intelligence giants including Anthropic and OpenAI. It's made by just three main companies: Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron.

The Commerce Department and CXMT didn't immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

Those same three companies control the lion's share of the DRAM market, but CXMT has made inroads. The company's share of the DRAM market jumped from 4% to 10% by revenue in a year, from the second quarter of 2025 to the same quarter of 2026, according to Counterpoint using the most recent reporting available. And in July, the company went public and its stock price soared, making it the most valuable company listed in China at the time.

While the company's HBM capabilities are nascent, according to Counterpoint, Reuters reported that the company was to begin production of the chips, even though yields are low at just 20% to 30%. That means that the majority of the chips it produces are faulty.

CXMT is already on the Pentagon's "1260H" list, which identifies several dozen Chinese companies with ties to Beijing's military. But Chan says that list doesn't come with stringent restrictions for U.S. businesses.

"It's not a list that has that much teeth to it, in terms of restrictions to U.S. companies working with Chinese firms on that list," Chan said.

The Wall Street Journal reported Aug. 9 that Apple was considering using CXMT's memory in iPhones and Macs in light of the global shortage driven by the AI buildout. Being on the Entity List could change that.

"From the U.S. government standpoint, their concern is that working with CXMT would – even as a customer, just buying their products – would help to improve their ability to ramp up memory chip production, and also maybe help China get further down the road towards HBM," Chan said. "It's part of the broader effort to stymie or slow down China's own chip development."

Cotton alluded to CXMT's efforts to break into the HBM market in his letter.

"[CXMT's] decision to move forward in less than ideal conditions highlights both Communist China's desperation for chips and manufacturing struggles without advanced equipment and knowledge," he wrote. "More can be done to ensure that CXMT is cut off from the financial and technological resources that aid its supply chain and undercut American growth."

#2879. Ukraine warns of fresh Starshield disruptions as U.S. envoys arrive in Moscow for talks with Putin

President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Ukraine was investigating what he described as fresh restrictions on SpaceX's Starshield service over the country, after similar speed limitations were temporarily imposed last month.

"Our units have been recording significant disruptions to Starshield's operations over the territory of Ukraine for several days now – along the entire front line and in the temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine," he wrote on X early Thursday.

Starshield is SpaceX's satellite network for government and national-security use, built on Starlink technology.

CNBC has reached out to SpaceX and the White House for comment.

"The Ukrainian military have already investigated the circumstances, and these are not random errors but systematic speed restrictions," he said, adding that they are trying to establish the circumstances with the U.S.

Zelenskyy said the restrictions imposed an effective speed limit on terminals moving at speeds above 161 kilometers per hour (100 miles per hour), disconnecting anything that was moving faster and making it harder for Ukraine to intercept Russian jet-powered drones.

"There have already been strikes that got through because our interception systems cannot reach sufficient speeds and are unable to intercept in time."

On Oct. 14, Ukrainian officials accused SpaceX of undermining the country's ability to defend itself by cutting connectivity speeds and thereby disrupting military operations. On October 24, Zelenskyy confirmed that systems had returned to their previous operating speeds.

According to The Wall Street Journal, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk ordered those restrictions, rendering some Ukrainian strike and interceptor drones virtually unusable. The newspaper later reported that U.S. President Donald Trump had asked Musk to reverse the move. Trump has denied that the White House ordered Starlink restrictions in Ukraine.

Musk initially appeared to pin responsibility for any restrictions on the U.S. government, writing on X that "Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the US government." He later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service, prompting the company to restrict its access to the high-speed satellite technology, something that Ukrainian officials have strongly contested.

Serhii Beskrestnov, a defense tech advisor to Zelenskyy, said at the time that Ukraine cannot use commercial Starlink for medium- or long-range drone strikes because the service does not operate over Russian territory and had imposed drone speed limits of 70–90 kilometers per hour for months.

On Thursday, Beskrestnov confirmed that "the speed of Starlink operation during movement was again limited."

"We did not rejoice for long," he said, adding that "any terms can be changed and discussed, instead of leaving our country without sky protection."

The comments came as U.S.-brokered peace talks wrapped up in Abu Dhabi on Wednesday and U.S. special envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner arrived in Moscow for further talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Witkoff and Kushner were met by Russian presidential envoy Kirill Dmitriev as they arrived at Vnukovo Airport on Thursday morning, according to Russian state news agency Tass. The pair were expected to discuss a lasting resolution to Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, which has now raged for more than four-and-a-half years.

Later on Thursday, Witkoff and Kushner are poised to meet with Zelenskyy on the sidelines of a political summit held in Ireland.

Trilateral talks in the United Arab Emirates between Ukrainian, Russian and American negotiators ended earlier this week without a major diplomatic breakthrough on a ceasefire or territorial settlement.

#2878. Diving deeper into Cavs 115-107 win over Knicks: James Harden showed why he’s integral to Cleveland’s success

For one night, the Cleveland Cavaliers exorcised their demons at Madison Square Garden. They came away with an impressive 115-107 win over the defending champion New York Knicks.

32 points on 9-for-14 shooting, 4 assists, 2 rebounds, 2 steals

Few players in NBA history have been able to single-handedly raise an offense's floor and ceiling like Harden. His ability to score from anywhere, manipulate defenders with his body, and make every pass made him nearly unguardable in his prime — while also allowing him to remain effective into his late 30s. However, that offensive consistency hasn't been there yet for the Cavs at the start of the season.

Harden needs his scoring and playmaking to work in concert with each other if he's going to elevate an offense. When he's scoring well, he draws the attention of the entire defense, which leaves teammates open for easy shots. But when he isn't able to create space off the dribble or connect on the outside shot, defenders can stay home with their assignments. And when they are staying home, those openings for Harden to set up teammates just aren't there. Everything is based on his scoring.

Over the last handful of games, we saw just how bad things can look when Harden isn't scoring. Defenses don't have to respect him and send multiple players his way. Things then become stagnant, and the entire offense suffers because of it. Against the Knicks, we witnessed the inverse.

The difference in this one was shotmaking. Harden found a scoring rhythm in the second quarter with his outside shot. This caused his defender to guard him much closer on the perimeter, making it easier for him to get inside the paint. The Knicks chose not to send multiple defenders his way, which allowed him to continue his effective scoring and close this game out with nine clutch fourth-quarter points.

It's not too much of a stretch to say that the success of the entire team depends on Harden. Everything offensively rises and falls with his play. The Cavs need to see this version of Harden much more often than they have.

21 points on 7-of-11 shoooting, 11 rebounds, 8 assists

Towns is playing the best basketball of his career. His unique skill set allows him to affect the game in so many different ways. He proved that again here with his outside shooting, playmaking, and rebounding.

The Cavs bigs tried to contain Towns — his offense was just better than their defense.

23 points on 8-of-15 shooting, 7 rebounds, 6 assists, 2 steals

Mitchell provided timely scoring while also being a good decision-maker. He correctly got the ball out of his hands and to his teammates when New York threw doubles at him. And his teammates rewarded him for doing so with the win.

Cleveland also did much better at controlling the glass. Head coach Kenny Atkinson has talked about rebounding being something everyone on the court needs to be involved in. Mitchell chipping in went a long way in making sure rebounding wasn't an issue.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can also shop all of Homage's Cavs gear HERE. The link to the Donovan Mitchell NBA Jam shirt is HERE.

Combined stats: 22 points on 7-of-20 shooting, 10 rebounds, 7 assists

The Knicks' incredible championship run was partially fueled by phenomenal play from their three starting wings. Likewise, their inability to show up to this game is a big reason why the Cavs were able to come away with a win.

Anunoby was helpful defensively, but couldn't find the range from the field, going 3-for-9. Bridges played just 17 minutes and was benched down the stretch due to being a complete non-factor. And Hart couldn't find his shot and wasn't an impactful rebounder.

Swing players often decide games against two high-powered teams like this. In this contest, New York's supporting pieces just didn't show up.

14 points on 6-of-11 shooting, 4 rebounds, 2 steals, 1 assist

We've yet to encounter a moment that was too big for Thomas. He played with a confidence that's rare to see in a rookie making his debut at MSG against the defending champions.

Thomas is first and foremost a scorer. We saw that again as he was able to create off the dribble and knock down open threes when the ball swung to him on the perimeter.

Atkinson has been able to continue putting Thomas in the rotation because of how he can positively influence the game in areas besides scoring. Defensively, he did a good job of guarding his man, didn't get lost off-ball, and forced two steals. Thomas was also effective at attacking the offensive glass as he came away with three offensive rebounds.

Thomas is scoring, but he's also doing a lot of the little things right that you need to in order to be a rotation player. This was another good outing for a rookie who has made nights like this feel routine.

15 points on 6-of-12 shooting, 3 rebounds, 1 assist

Proctor showed the wide array of skills that he has. He initiated the offense as a secondary ball-handler, knocked down outside shots when the ball swung to him, and was helpful defensively.

This was exactly what you want to see from someone who's trying to prove that he should stay in the rotation when Sam Merril and Nae'Qwan Tomlin return from injury.

7 points on 2-of-12 shooting, 5 rebounds, 4 blocks, 4 assists

Watson's defensive effort made up for his poor shooting. He was seemingly everywhere on defense and secured the victory with a chase-down block on Deuce McBride.

The Knicks only got 24.4% of their missed shots (21st percentile) while the Cavs grabbed 32.6% of available offensive rebounds (65th percentile).

The Cavs controlled the glass, won the possession battle, and ultimately won the game.

This game doesn't fix all that has been wrong with the Cavaliers at the start of the season. Nor does it show that they're going to put the pieces together and become the team they have the potential to be. It does, however, demonstrate that the Cavs have the talent to beat anyone.

The Cavs were impressive on both ends of the floor. Offensively, their two elite guards controlled the game while still making way for role players like Proctor and Thomas to make meaningful contributions.

Defensively, Jarrett Allen and Evan Mobley made it difficult to score inside and kept the Knicks off the free-throw line. Watson also made a sizeable impact as a point-of-attack defender and weak-side rim protector. This resulted in New York becoming more of an outside-shooting team than they would've liked.

In short, this was a complete performance.

The Cavs have had other impressive wins this season, but they haven't been able to back any of them up with anything substantive. At this point, we don't know what team we'll see on a nightly basis, and there's no evidence that they're creating sustainable habits that will help them in the postseason. This win will only be different if they can actually build off it from a process and results standpoint.

I biancocelesti guidano la classifica nonostante l’Olimpico vuoto per la protesta dei tifosi contro Lotito.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

#2876. Scott Colom wins Mississippi Senate election over incumbent Republican Hyde-Smith

Democratic nominee Scott Colom won the Mississippi Senate election over Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, the Associated Press reported Wednesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, will be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

His projected victory — which was not considered likely by national analysts before Election Day — is the latest in a series of stunning wins by Democrats this midterm cycle.

Two sources close to Colom's campaign said Hyde-Smith called him late Wednesday afternoon to concede.

"While this is not the outcome we worked for, or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters," Hyde-Smith said in a video posted on X.

"I want to congratulate Scott Colom and wish him well as he takes on the tremendous responsibility of representing our great state," Hyde-Smith said.

Democrats now will have a majority of at least 55 seats in the Senate, having flipped at least eight seats from Republicans.

Alaska is the only remaining Senate election to be called. In that state, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola, a former House member, has a narrow lead over Sen. Dan Sullivan, the Republican incumbent.

"Blue Mississippi is here," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a post on X.

"Scott Colom is the only person who could've gotten this done. I knew it from the moment I met him," Schumer wrote.

In addition to the Senate, Democrats have won more than enough seats to take control of the House of Representatives when the next Congress begins in January.

#2875. Will the Broncos make any changes to the starting lineup over the bye week?

The Denver Broncos are 7-2 and hold the AFC West lead for now. That record is impressive all things considered. If you would have told me that would have been their record before the bye, I would have respectfully disagreed given the gauntlet of opponents they have played to date.

There is no doubt the Broncos have a lot of room for improvement. They haven't been at their best but are certainly trending in the right direction. Over the next week, Denver will have the opportunity to rest, recuperate, and also do some reflection on what they've accomplished this season. Moreover, what they need to do moving forward to have the best shot at earning the franchise's fourth Lombardi Trophy.

Yesterday, Head Coach Sean Payton spoke with Colin Cowherd on the status of the team. If you haven't had a chance to watch the interview, you certainly should at the link above. One of big questions was Cowherd asked for was Payton's assessment of the offense in the first half of the season.

"The cool thing about the bye right now is you really get a chance and zoom, and look really close what's been going well, what hasn't, and why hasn't it been—and let's not be kidding ourselves," stated Payton.

He went on to exert extreme confidence in Bo Nix. The third-year quarterback hasn't made the huge jump fans were expecting this year. Nevertheless, Payton was adamant that the team has the right signal caller behind center. Despite some struggles, he is happy with what he has seen from Nix going up against some very tough defenses.

Payton went on to talk about what they can do to set up Nix for better success in the second part of the season. Without hesitation, he discussed a position group that has experienced some regression this season at a few positions—the offensive line.

"I'm watching the cutups from the game we just played [versus the Carolina Panthers]. We didn't block them very well. I'm just being honest—that has to improve. We have plenty of depth on the offensive line. During this bye, we're going to look at all those little things closely," added Payton.

I'm certainly not an offensive line expert by any means. Though I think it's safe to say the Broncos have only had two consistent starters in their offensive trenches. Those would be left tackle Garett Bolles and right guard Quinn Meinerz. When it comes to center Luke Wattenberg, left guard Ben Powers, and right tackle Mike McGlinchey—all three have struggled and haven't been at their best this season.

I'd be skeptical that the Broncos would consider benching McGlinchey. I also find it hard to believe they would bench Wattenberg for Alex Forsyth. However, I wouldn't be surprised if Powers was replaced. Last season when he got hurt, Powers was replaced by Alex Palcewzki and he performed admirably. Well enough for Denver to give him a multi-year extension this offseason. Powers had a restructure that lowered his base salary significantly but upped his guarantees.

As noted in a feed post yesterday, Powers has struggled this season. In nine games, Powers has been credited with 14 blown blocks per SIS Data Hub. Ten of those have been pass blocks with four of them credit against the run. Though based on the eye test, I'd say they are drastically underestimating his issues paving lanes for the Broncos' backs in the run game.

We'll just have to wait and see if the Broncos wind up making a change on their offensive line. Based on Payton's discussion with Cowherd, that seems like a legitimate possibility. Denver certainly has to be better up front in the trenches if they want to be top-level competitors the rest of the way.

What do you think, Broncos Country? Should Denver make a change on the offensive line? If so, what position and who do you feel should be inserted into the starting lineup. Sound off in the comments section and let me know.

#2874. As credit card balances hit a record high, medical bills can become 'shadow debt,' expert says

Total household debt rose $334 billion in the third quarter, to $19.1 trillion, according to new data from the Federal Reserve Bank of New York. That total doesn't include medical debt — at least, not directly.

An estimated $220 billion in medical debt exists, according to an analysis of 2021 federal survey data by KFF, a national nonprofit healthcare research group. But medical expenses can become hard-to-track "shadow" debt if consumers pay them with a credit card or other forms of borrowing, said Jim Triggs, the CEO of Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling organization.

"Technically, as soon as that consumer pays that debt off with another mechanism, now it becomes credit card debt, or housing debt, or whatever mechanism you used to pay it off," Triggs said.

He said 20% of MMI's debt and budget counseling clients in 2025 cited a medical hardship as the reason they went into debt.

Separately, about 17% of households surveyed in 2024 reported still owing money on a loan or credit card used to pay a medical bill, according to a 2025 study by researchers at the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Here's a look at more stories on how to manage, grow and protect your money for the years ahead.

Americans' credit card debt rose to a record $1.29 trillion in the third quarter, the New York Fed found, while home equity lines of credit saw their biggest quarterly jump since 2005, to $481 billion. Those increases come amid broader affordability pressures for consumers, including pricier everyday expenses amid persistent inflation.

It is "kind of hard to parse" how much of Americans' debt load comes from medical expenses, said Julie Margetta Morgan, president of The Century Foundation, a New York-based progressive nonprofit think tank.

"There's a big piece of medical debt that is about people being on high-deductible health plans, so they have insurance, but they're still getting these big medical bills that they can't pay," Margetta Morgan said.

Nationwide, among households with medical debt, the median amount in collections is $1,492, according to the Urban Institute. The nonprofit research organization's report drew the data from more than 10 million anonymized credit bureau records from August 2026.

Jessica Colon has used credit cards to pay medical debt. She and her husband were both self-employed at the time with a high-deductible health insurance plan. Colon, now 50, says the debt started with an unexpected $16,000 hospital bill for her son's care in the mid-2010s.

"Because I could barely keep up with that payment, I kept charging on my credit card all my monthly payments, and that's where it all compounded," Colon said. "I was just drowning."

The credit card debt grew to more than $45,000 before she turned to a nonprofit credit counselor, who negotiated lower rates and put together a five-year plan to pay it off. "It's embarrassing, honestly, how much money was interest, and then of course you're building the interest on the interest," Colon said. She zeroed out that debt in early 2024.

Before taking on debt, experts advise reviewing medical bills and billing codes for errors, including duplicate charges.

Consumers should also ask medical providers if they offer assistance before signing up for a medical credit card, experts say. Many nonprofit hospitals are required by law to have a written financial assistance plan.

"A lot of nonprofit hospitals are not actually offering this plan to people, and in many cases, they're offering them a medical credit card before they offer that financial assistance," said Margetta Morgan, a former associate director of research, monitoring and regulations at the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Colon said she wishes she had tried to negotiate the medical bills before putting the balance on a high-interest credit card. "You don't want to confront the place that's helping your child. So I didn't realize that I could do that," she said.

— CNBC's Sharon Epperson contributed reporting.

#2873. Unions helped Democrats win big in 2026. Now they want action

When Brody Rutledge wasn't working 60-hour weeks or raising his four children this year, he was busy helping secure political victories for Democrats.

The 25-year-old was part of a group of Ohio union members who planned social-media blitzes in support of Amy Acton's campaign for governor and Sherrod Brown's effort to reclaim a Senate seat. On his lunch hour, during break times and after his kids went to sleep, the electrician helped create posts and videos to help raise the profiles of the two candidates among blue-collar voters.

The posts attracted more than 15,000 views.

Rutledge's efforts paid off: Acton and Brown won their respective races, part of the blue wave that swept Democrats to power in Washington and in state houses across America — including in many states, like Ohio, that had been considered Republican-leaning, or reliably Republican.

"People are realizing that there needs to be a change," Rutledge told CNBC. "We are extremely excited with the results."

Rutledge was a part of what organized labor considered one of its biggest political efforts yet. Unions spent millions of dollars and mobilized thousands of members to get out the vote for Democrats in the midterm election. Now, they expect newly elected politicians to deliver.

Democrats, they say, need to get a handle on inflation, enact worker protections and deliver union jobs.

The AFL-CIO launched a seven-figure paid media campaign for the election and said its members since Labor Day had engaged with nearly 15 million voters. By the AFL-CIO's count, more than 600 members of unions themselves were elected in the midterm, including dozens heading to Washington and hundreds who are bound for statehouses.

The CNBC Voter Poll shows midterm voters who were union members or lived in a union household went for Democrats over Republicans by a margin of 22 percentage points, the highest of the last four election cycles. That's more than double the margin for the rest of the electorate: When looking at voters in non-union households, the gap shrunk to 10 percentage points.

"We are very proud of the impact that the labor movement made on this election," said Liz Shuler, president of the AFL-CIO, a federation of unions that represents almost 15 million workers. "It was our biggest and boldest effort ever."

In some cases, unions got involved before party nominees had been decided.

In Michigan, the United Auto Workers organized behind Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead of what became a contentious primary race for U.S. Senate that he eventually won against Haley Stevens. The union believes that about one out of every 10 early voters in the August primary were UAW members.

"It was incredibly impactful," Connor Farrell, a progressive fundraiser who worked with El-Sayed, said of the UAW's endorsement. "I'm not sure we would have won without them."

El-Sayed and Jocelyn Benson — the Democratic nominee for Michigan governor, who also had early backing from the UAW — cruised to victory in last week's general election. It was a sharp reversal for the Wolverine state, which backed President Donald Trump in 2016 and 2024.

The UAW held a rally on the eve of Election Day headlined by El-Sayed and Benson, along with other Democrats running for statewide office. The Detroit-based union said its volunteers knocked on more than 100,000 doors and made half a million phone calls for the election.

The CNBC Voter Poll showed union households made up about 22% of the electorate in Michigan in the 2026 midterm, above the 16% mean nationwide. Union households in Michigan broke for El-Sayed and Benson by higher-than-average margins of 25 and 27 points, respectively.

"There's a direct correlation between a ballot box and a bread box," said UAW President Shawn Fain, who described the union's 2026 political efforts as the largest in his lifetime. "What we bargain for today can be taken away in the legislative halls tomorrow."

In Nevada, another swing state, members of AFL-CIO unions canvassed for a list of candidates including Aaron Ford, the Democrat who unseated Republican Gov. Joe Lombardo. Down ballot, the AFL-CIO endorsed candidates in state assembly, mayoral and city council races.

Vladimir Ortega, a member of the National Association of Letter Carriers living in North Las Vegas, said displeasure with how Republicans had managed the economy led him to volunteer to canvas with the AFL-CIO for the first time. The 34-year-old met Ford and other candidates at political events, and he plans to volunteer for candidates backed by AFL-CIO again in 2028.

"People felt like they had to be there," Ortega said. "Their money hurt, and they want to do something about it."

For generations, union voters and their families were a core bloc of the Democratic base. But the party's iron grip on organized labor had slipped in recent election cycles as union members, like other working-class voters, grew frustrated by high inflation and affordability challenges, according to Paul Clark, a Pennsylvania State University professor.

The National Election Pool's Poll showed union households voted for Democrats by a margin of 16 and 15 percentage points in the 2020 and 2022 contests, respectively. That narrowed to just 8 points in 2024, when voters' economic frustrations led Republicans to win a trifecta of power in Washington: the presidency and both houses of Congress.

The Teamsters declined to back either Trump or former Vice President Kamala Harris in 2024, snapping a long history of Democratic endorsements. The union, with more than one million members, said internal polling showed members preferred Trump over Harris.

Teamsters General President Sean O'Brien said on social media that he voted for Ashley Moody, the victorious Republican candidate for U.S. Senate in Florida, in the 2026 midterm. The Teamsters didn't respond to a CNBC request for comment.

"Obviously, 2024 was very disappointing for unions and the American labor movement," said Clark at Penn State, who has authored books on collective bargaining and organized labor. "That caused some introspection on the part of unions in terms of their political strategies and how they communicate with their members about politics."

This time around, there appeared to be more excitement among card-carrying union members heading to the polls. AFL-CIO polling showed 83% of union voters were "very motivated" to vote, an increase of 10 points from the last midterm election in 2022.

Following their midterm defeat, Clark said he wouldn't be surprised to see Republicans courting unions ahead of the 2028 presidential election. But that could be an uphill battle, given the party's history of advocating for business interests over workers, Clark said.

Looking ahead, organized labor has big expectations for Democrats after helping the party to power in Washington and state capitols across the country.

In Ohio, Rutledge said that his International Brotherhood of Electric Workers chapter is focused on ensuring new data center construction projects use union labor. In Michigan, UAW President Fain said the union expects a majority-Democrat state legislature to approve a law guaranteeing unemployment insurance for striking workers.

"The days of just blindly giving our endorsements to people because of party affiliation are over," Fain said. "They're going to earn our support."

If Democrats don't act when they have legislative majorities in Washington, union leaders warn that they will pull their support in 2028 and beyond.

Across the nation, Shuler, the AFL-CIO president, said union voters went to the polls last week in hopes of enacting policy that will level the economic playing field. The CNBC Voter Poll found that 70% of respondents believed the U.S. economy favors the wealthy over the middle- and working class.

"Republican, Democrat, independent, we'll be with you if you're with us," Shuler said. "It just so happens that we had a big wave of Democratic candidates who were talking about working-class issues."

#2872. Cleveland Browns news: Silencing the QB noise

Cleveland Browns head coach Todd Monken is still new to town, so he can be excused for not understanding how things work around here when it comes to quarterbacks.

Every decision relating to the position must be questioned and defended on a daily basis. It does not matter how many names are on the infamous quarterback jersey; the mob always wants another one added to it.

Because where is the fun of simply knowing who the starter is and practicing for that week's opponent?

Monken appears to be catching on, however, as he showed during Wednesday's announcement that quarterback Deshaun Watson would be back as the starter for this week's game against the Houston Texans after missing one game with a concussion.

It is the type of standard move that NFL teams make all the time. A starter misses a game (or games) with an injury, and once healthy, they return to their former status.

Maybe the fact that Monken is trying his best to run the Browns like a real NFL team is throwing people off. After all, despite not playing well for a full game yet this season, Watson has been instrumental in helping the Browns stay relevant into November, which is a rare feat around these parts.

Why would Monken want to go away from that, especially after watching the same script play out against New Orleans only to end with a crucial loss instead of a key win?

The answer, as it has been since the start of the season, is that he never wanted to, and it was only Watson suffering a concussion that brought a conversation no one wanted to have back into the light.

Now that Monken has answered the question, the team can focus on getting ready for the Houston Texans and attempt to avoid going into the bye week on a three-game losing streak.

#2871. FIGC, Galliani e Malagò: «un’elezione nuovamente annullabile»

Il legale Lattanzi chiede di sospendere il provvedimento: le verifiche tornerebbero al Segretario Generale.

Adriano Galliani ha presentato ricorso al Tribunale Federale Nazionale della FIGC contro l’istituzione della commissione incaricata di esaminare le candidature alla presidenza federale. Secondo quanto riportato da Onefootball, La commissione è stata voluta dal commissario Giovanni Malagò, a sua volta in corsa. La contestazione riguarda anche il sostegno del Genoa: se la firma del club a favore di Malagò fosse annullata, il commissario non raggiungerebbe la quota minima richiesta tra le società di Serie A.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il ricorso mette in discussione l’affidamento delle verifiche alla commissione istituita su iniziativa di Malagò. I dettagli, riferiti da Gianluca Di Marzio e ripresi dall’ANSA, sono riportati da Onefootball.

Secondo il legale Lattanzi, che in mattinata ha rassegnato le dimissioni dalla commissione agenti FIGC, il ricorso richiede un intervento prima che il procedimento elettorale prosegua. Lattanzi sostiene che il pregiudizio per Galliani, legato alla possibilità di competere ad armi pari e di ottenere una valutazione imparziale delle candidature, non sarebbe sanabile successivamente.

Al centro del reclamo c’è la duplice sottoscrizione del Genoa. Il club aveva inizialmente sostenuto Galliani, che aveva inserito quell’adesione negli atti formalizzati. In seguito ha cambiato posizione, schierandosi a favore di Malagò attraverso una PEC di revoca e diffida.

L’eventuale annullamento del sostegno del Genoa a Malagò avrebbe un effetto sulla sua candidatura: senza quella firma, il commissario non arriverebbe alle undici sottoscrizioni minime dei club di Serie A. Il termine per presentare le candidature è fissato al 14 novembre.
