#2771. Warriors vs. Rockets player grades: Yaxel Lendeborg steps up as Steph Curry hurt

Well. That was certainly a night of basketball. A whole lot happened on Wednesday. The Golden State Warriors fell behind the Houston Rockets by 15 points at the end of the first quarter, then came roaring back to tie it. Steph Curry missed most of the game with an abdominal injury that required an MRI. Charles Bassey was punched by Marcus Smart. And the Warriors saw their eight-game winning streak come to a halt, losing 115-108 to a Rockets team that was significantly healthier.

You can't fault the Warriors for losing, given all of that. But grades still need to be handed out, so here they are. As is always the case, grades are based on my expectations for each player, with a "B" grade representing the average performance for that player.

Note: True-shooting percentage (TS) is a scoring efficiency metric that accounts for threes and free throws. Entering Wednesday's games, league-average TS was 57.2%.

36 minutes, 25 points, 10 rebounds, 2 steals, 2 turnovers, 2 fouls, 9-for-19 shooting, 2-for-7 threes, 5-for-8 free throws, 55.5% TS, -15

It's a testament to Lendeborg that the Warriors lost Curry and still overcame a deficit to give themselves a chance to win. It's a testament to Lendeborg that, even with Kevin Durant, Amen Thompson, Fred VanVleet, and Alperen Şengün on the other side, he comfortably led both squads in scoring. It's a testament that, in the on-court post-game interview, ESPN decided to ask Durant about Lendeborg specifically. And it's a testament that Durant called him a "problem," meant clearly as a compliment.

Just another 25 and 10 for Lendeborg, as those numbers are very near his season averages, and mark the third double-double of his young career.

Lendeborg was solid through and through, and then some. The biggest issue with his game was that, for the first time, his jumper didn't look good. The threes he missed were really clanked, and he didn't look all that comfortable at the free throw line, either. But as has been the case all year, his resolve, patience, and savvy were stunning. A misaligned jumper neither scared him from scoring, nor made him press. He just kept making the right play, and attacking.

It wasn't a very good playmaking game for him, in part because Houston's length and athleticism seemed to bother him a bit … not in a way that was at all concerning, but just in a "oh yeah, they don't make them like that in the Big 10" way. On the flip side, his defense was pretty spectacular in this game, both on his opening assignment of Jabari Smith Jr., and also on the many possessions where he was matched up with Durant. Speaking of which, those two put on a show in the second quarter, with Lendeborg going toe to toe with one of the greatest scorers in league history.

Grade: A-Post-game bonus: Led the team in points and rebounds.

27 minutes, 7 points, 5 rebounds, 7 assists, 1 block, 1 turnover, 2 fouls, 3-for-5 shooting, 1-for-1 threes, 70.0% TS, -20

As long as we're talking about great defensive performances … Draymond Green, folks. What a defender. Green drew an opening assignment on Durant, someone he has historically guarded very well (relatively speaking … great offense beats great defense, and KD is great offense). Dray did a tremendous job on KD (who still dropped in 20 points, it's worth noting), and it was just so much fun to watch one of this generation's top scorers go at one of this generation's top defenders. Don't take it for granted!

And here's your latest update on how in control Green has been to start the year: 75 assists, 29 turnovers, 32 shot attempts.

Grade: A-Post-game bonus: Led the team in assists; tied for the worst plus/minus on the team.

21 minutes, 2 points, 1 rebound, 4 assists, 3 fouls, 1-for-7 shooting, 0-for-5 threes, 14.3% TS, -10

Not the best night for Big Al. He got his first start of the year so that Golden State could have some extra size against a huge Houston squad. He struggled a bit against Şengün, and really couldn't get anything going of his own on offense. He didn't have any bad plays, per se, but it wasn't an impactful game on the glass or on defense for him which, when paired with a jump shot that wasn't landing, made for a fairly poor game.

37 minutes, 19 points, 6 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 steals, 1 turnover, 3 fouls, 6-for-14 shooting, 3-for-10 threes, 4-for-5 free throws, 58.6% TS, -20

Like Lendeborg, Podziemski was a huge part of why the Warriors were able to crawl back into the game even with their superstar ruled out. He seemed to always have a big shot up his sleeve when the team needed it most … which is something we've really grown accustomed to with Podz.

As with all his teammates, Podziemski had a huge challenge with his opening assignment, as he was defensively matched up against Thompson. He performed admirably at times, though there's only so much you can do when the player you're tasked with stopping is significantly larger than you and arguably the best athlete in the entire league. Sometimes you just gotta tip your cap.

Grade: B+Post-game bonus: Tied for the worst plus/minus on the team.

6 minutes, 3 points, 1 turnover, 1-for-3 shooting, 1-for-2 threes, 50.0% TS, -9

Curry played fewer than six minutes, but those minutes were more physical than any other game he's played this year. He had hard collisions with Rockets players on numerous occasions, and hit the deck hard on a few others. Nothing dirty, just strong, physical, high-quality defense of which he became collateral damage. He left halfway through the first quarter holding his side, and was ruled out for the rest of the game with abdominal soreness. We'll hold our breath as we wait for the results of his MRI.

12 minutes, 0 points, 3 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 block, 4 turnovers, 0-for-3 shooting, 0-for-2 threes, 0.0% TS, +7

Santos' stats look quite bad. He didn't score, and he turned the ball over four times in a relatively short amount of time. He had some really nice possessions and plays though, especially on the defensive end. But the Rockets, like many athletic teams, just gave Santos too many fits, and a turnover every three minutes just won't fly.

25 minutes, 11 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 3 fouls, 3-for-7 shooting, 3-for-7 threes, 2-for-2 free throws, 69.8% TS, -8

Melton is such a key contributor off of the bench, but really, games like this are where his true value is felt. Sure, running the second unit is important, but it's absolutely critical that the Dubs have a strong veteran presence to step in when Curry is unavailable. As soon as Curry left the court, Melton's role grew bigger, so much so that he was in the starting five for the second half.

And while no one can do a Steph impression, Melton certainly filled in admirable. He ran the offense better than the three assists would suggest, and made a trio of important threes to keep the Dubs in the game. His defense was once again strong, and he steadied a ship that was at risk of tipping.

In other words, he earned his paycheck with this game.

12 minutes, 7 points, 6 rebounds, 1 block, 1 foul, 3-for-4 shooting, 1-for-2 free throws, 71.7% TS, +2

Let's start with the most impressive thing: Bassey had a punch thrown at his face by Smart and walked away unbothered. His reflexes were, thankfully, fast enough that the violent punch ended up being a glancing blow rather than landing squarely, but even so, you would not only excuse, but expect a player to react angrily when another player throws a haymaker at their face. No one would be blaming Bassey if he went right back at Smart for that inexcusable move, but can you imagine the ramifications of if he did? A full-on fight could have broken out, which could have resulted in Bassey and possibly some teammates getting ejected, or even suspended. And with the amount of sidelined players the Dubs currently have, they simply could not have afforded that.

Bassey walked away like nothing happened, and he deserves so much praise for that. It's not easy to turn the other cheek, especially in the heart of competition, but Bassey did exactly that.

He also played a damn good basketball game, which I suppose is also fairly important.

19 minutes, 5 points, 5 rebounds, 1-for-7 shooting, 1-for-3 threes, 2-for-3 free throws, 30.1% TS, -12

I do not think that Niang would have walked away from being punched by Smart, but you know what? It takes all kinds. The Warriors contain multitudes, and we love that about them.

Not the best game for Niang, who suffered a bit both from playing such an athletic team, and from not getting to share the court with Curry, as those two have really developed quite a rapport (and Niang greatly benefits from the spacing that Curry's gravity creates). He had good rebounding and okay defense, but not much else.

19 minutes, 9 points, 4 rebounds, 1 steal, 2 turnovers, 2 fouls, 3-for-6 shooting, 2-for-3 threes, 1-for-2 free throws, 65.4% TS, -2

Richard deserves a whole lot of credit for his toughness. He missed just one game due to UCL sprain in his thumb, when I assumed he'd miss a few weeks, and possibly a few months. Would Richard have taken the time off for surgery or a longer healing process if the Warriors were not so banged up? Who knows. Either way, it showed great toughness that he was out there on the court with his teammates.

And you certainly wouldn't know he was hurting by his performance. His defense was excellent, and he was so aggressive on offense. He's really started attending the Gary Payton II school of cutting, and his jump shot looks great this year as well. Strong game.

18 minutes, 13 points, 1 rebound, 1 assist, 2 fouls, 4-for-9 shooting, 3-for-8 threes, 2-for-2 free throws, 65.8% TS, +15

For the second game in a row, Cryer was called into emergency duty after a guard got injured. On Monday it was Brandon Williams; on Wednesday it was Curry. He was okay in that last-minute role change against the New Orleans Pelicans, but he was excellent against the Rockets. He came into the game in the first quarter and immediately made a difference on offense, with some aggressive dribbling and some slick shooting. His offense really is quite impressive, and while the two-way contract is buried on the depth chart, it's clear that his coaches and teammates trust him, and that he has a lot to offer.

Grade: APost-game bonus: Best plus/minus on the team.

4 minutes, 4 points, 1 assist, 1 foul, 1-for-2 shooting, 0-for-1 threes, 2-for-2 free throws, 69.4% TS, +11

A little garbage time for Ike, though he played a huge role in trying to make it no longer garbage time.

#2770. NC State moves even closer to hiring Philip Rivers

Don’t expect Philip Rivers to come out of retirement again this season. He’ll be busy.

Via ESPN, the NC State board of trustees is expected to finalize a contract that will make Rivers the next head coach of the Wolfpack.

Rivers played for NC State from 1999 through 2003. He was the fourth overall pick in the 2004 draft, technically of the Giants. He was traded to the Chargers (with Eli Manning going to New York), and Rivers played for the Chargers through 2019. He spent 2020 with the Colts, and he unexpectedly returned late in the 2025 season to play for the Colts again.

Rivers became a high-school coach in Alabama in 2021. He has never coached at the college or pro level, but he interviewed for the Bills’ head-coaching job.

It’s not known whether he still aspires to coach at the NFL level. As he gets experience at NC State — and if he does well — buzz could build for a shot with an NFL team.

For now, he’ll be returning to the place where he played college football. His name surfaced almost immediately after long-time coach Dave Doeren was fired last month. Along the way, NC State interviewed fellow former NC State quarterback (and NFL quarterback and current CBS analyst) Russell Wilson.

#2769. Dolphins news: Injuries, roster changes, and preparation for Colts in Week 10

The Miami Dolphins are preparing for their Week 10 game against the Indianapolis Colts, which kicks off at 1 p.m. Eastern on Sunday. The game features two teams in contention for the first overall pick in the 2027 NFL Draft, though the Colts do not hold a first-round pick this year. Instead, as part of their trade for cornerback Sauce Gardner last year, that pick belongs to the New York Jets. Could this game decide who holds the keys to the draft in the spring?

This game will also feature the Dolphins making multiple roster adjustments after injuries to quarterback Malik Willis and defensive tackle Zach Sieler. Kyle McCord is expected to start in Willis's place, his first regular-season opportunity after the Philadelphia Eagles selected him in the sixth round in 2025. Willis is out for at least four weeks, though season-ending surgery is still possible if his goin injury does not respond to rest.

Sieler tore his biceps muscle on the first play of the team's Week 9 game against the Detroit Lions, but played through the injury for the remainder of the game. He will need surgery and around six months of recovery time before he is cleared to return to football activities.

The Dolphins may also be without starting center Aaron Brewer, who missed Wednesday's practice with a concussion. He will have to clear the NFL's concussion protocol before he is cleared to play.

#2768. Andorra goalkeeper Jesus Owono undecided on football return after Jesus de Miguel’s tragic death

FC Andorra Carles Manso said on Thursday that goalkeeper Jesus Owono has not yet decided whether he will return to competition following the death of Tenerife forward Jesus de Miguel.

De Miguel died in hospital on 4 November, the day after a collision with Owono on the pitch during a Spanish football match.

Owono, who is Equatorial Guinea's goalkeeper, released a statement through Andorra last week in which he described the ordeal as "the most difficult days of my life."

Manso has now offered an update on Jesus Owono, telling a press conference: "What we have to do is support him in every way and be close to him.

"We will give him space so that he feels completely free to make any decision, whether he wants to return, when, or if he does not want to return."

Tenerife paid tribute to Jesus de Miguel over the weekend following his death last week (Getty)

Manso said Owono was spending time with his family, friends and partner and described the aftermath of last week's tragedy as a "very complicated situation to manage".

"When he feels it is appropriate and when he feels genuinely ready to take a decision like that (he will do so)," added Manso.

Owono has 47 caps for Equatorial Guinea. He was not called up for his country's doubleheader with Zimbabwe during this international break.

Andorra travel to Almeria on Saturday for their first fixture since De Miguel's death.

Over the weekend, the Tenerife players and thousands of fans paid an emotional final tribute to De Miguel at the club's stadium.

A massive banner displaying his black-and-white photograph and the words "Forever Demi", a reference to the player's nickname, hung behind a central pitch stage at the Estadio Heliodoro Rodriguez Lopez.

Following a video showing footage of his Tenerife debut, fans chanted: "De Miguel, we love you".

Players sat beside representatives from all clubs in Spain's top two divisions, including Real Madrid director Emilio Butragueno, as tears fell in the stands.

"To speak of you in the past tense is tremendously unfair," Tenerife captain Enric Gallego said, pausing repeatedly during his speech.

"It's still hard to believe that this has happened to us, Demi; you'll always be one of us."

"We're still struggling to come to terms with the fact that you won't be with us again."

#2766. Dianna Russini's return includes no specifics about multiple controversies

As expected, Dianna Russini made her return to sports media on Wednesday, with a one-hour show platformed by an illegal offshore sports book.

It’s hardly a prime gig, but her “crisis publicist” managed to create plenty of hype — and to finagle a puff piece with People.com that included no questions about the circumstances that prompted Russini’s resignation from The Athletic.

In September, the New York Post reported that Russini would “share her side of the scandal upon taking her next job.” She hasn’t, and she didn’t in the debut episode of her new show.

Technical difficulties delayed the release of the first episode, which was supposed to be live but seemed to be entirely pre-recorded. (The company blamed the glitch on a “record-breaking audience.” Comparable to what?)

Once the episode finally appeared, it featured three guests (none of whom are current players or coaches), preceded by a segment in which she addressed the future of Ravens quarterback Lamar Jackson. Which, from an editorial standpoint, is hardly the top story in the NFL on the day after the trade deadline.

There was nothing new or particularly insightful about Jackson. It was a summary of the facts that anyone who has been paying attention to the situation already knew, with a not-groundbreaking assertion that the Ravens want to keep him beyond 2026.

More significantly, the wooly mammoth in the mud room was not addressed. The overriding message was that she went away for a while, she went through some things, and now she’s back. For anyone who doesn’t know the story, she said nothing that would even begin to shed light on it.

If it’s going to be an opinions-and-interviews show, that’s fine. If she’s going to include the reporting of facts about the NFL, the credibility question can’t be ignored.

And this isn’t about the relationship (or whatever it was) with Patriots coach Mike Vrabel. It’s about how she responded to it.

She issued a strident denial when the Post published the photos of the two of them at a resort in Arizona. One week later, she abruptly resigned from her position at The Athletic, complaining that “commentators in various media have engaged in self-feeding speculation that is simply unmoored from the facts.”

In the months since making that assertion, she hasn’t shared her own version of the facts. In her kid-gloves interview with People.com, Russini said that the “lies became so out of control.” Without identifying even one of the alleged lies.

Her nothing-to-see-here position was undermined by Vrabel himself, who abandoned his initial position that the initial photos “show a completely innocent interaction and any suggestion otherwise is laughable.” Then came another group of photos, from March 2020, which had a distinct know-it-when-you-see-it vibe as to the specific nature of the relationship. That prompted Vrabel to step away from the team for the all-important third day of the draft, in order to attend counseling with his family.

So, to date, Russini’s official position is that it was all a big nothing. Vrabel’s words and actions make it clear that it was something, whatever it was. (That boat-trip video didn’t help much, either.)

Beyond the fact that The Athletic concluded, following an internal investigation, that “Russini’s relationship with Vrabel was in clear violation of The Athletic’s standards,” the situation creates clear questions of credibility. Put simply, and bluntly, can the audience believe what she says?

That leads to the other issue, which has been largely overlooked in connection with her return to sports media — if a one-hour show hosted by an illegal offshore sports book even counts as that. In February, she told the story of being pulled over for texting while driving. She claimed that she FaceTimed a current head coach to get her out of the ticket. And, based on the bodycam footage released by the local authorities, that contention was flat-out false.

Forget the relationship with Vrabel. All insiders have compromised their journalistic integrity, in one way or another. The interests of the audience often take a back seat to the broader chess match of getting and staying in position to get “scoops” (which usually come in the form of a five-minute heads-up before a transaction is announced). Punches are pulled. Relevant stories are overlooked or downplayed. Favors are done for sources.

It’s one thing to be compromised. It’s another thing to have real issues when it comes to telling the truth. Because the fundamental premise of anyone who is engaged in reporting of any kind is that they are at all times telling the truth.

Even in post-truth America, it’s a standard that should never be relaxed.

Russini’s credibility has been damaged by her denials regarding the relationship (whatever it was) with Vrabel, and by Vrabel’s actions that undercut her denials. Moreover, the casual, nonchalant spinning of a phony yarn about FaceTiming a coach to get out of a ticket makes it even harder to believe anything she says.

That’s why I said last Saturday on #PFTPM that we’ll be ignoring any of her reports until her credibility is somehow restored, if that’s even possible at this point. In this business, it would be very easy to make assertions and couch them as news from an unnamed source. When someone has not one but two concrete instances of clear issues when it comes to telling the truth, it would be irresponsible for us to blindly accept any reports from that reporter and to use them here.

It’s that simple. People may not like some of the things we report, but the goal at all times is to be fully and completely honest with the audience. Part of that duty of honesty includes being brutally honest that the word of someone who has, in multiple ways, damaged her own credibility simply can’t be trusted here.

#2765. Keenan Allen says his beef with Mike Evans is in the past

Keenan Allen says his beef with Mike Evans is in the past.

Allen joined Evans in the 49ers’ wide receivers room this week after a trade from the Colts.

“We squashed it a long time ago,” Allen said Wednesday, via David Bonilla of 49erswebzone.com. “Everybody wants to bring it back up, obviously. But no, Mike’s a hell of a player. He’s probably better than me at this point.”

In a memorable 2020 back-and-forth on social media, Allen and Evans engaged in a spirited debate about who was the better wide receiver.

Allen was ranked 77th on the NFL Top 100 that season. Tyreek Hill ranked 22nd, Evans 30th and Chris Godwin 38th. Allen argued that he was better than Hill, Evans and Godwin.

“Ok I’m tired of biten my tongue...@cheetah @MikeEvans13_ @chrisgoodwin... ( and the list goes on) ARE NOT a better receiver than me! Faster than me...every day of the week but separation..CHILD PLEASE!” Allen wrote in his post.

Evans responded, “You tagged the wrong Chris Godwin lol and don’t be mad at us we ain’t make the rankings or care about em. I like the confidence but be realistic you not on my level bro.”

Allen, who is in his 14th season, has 1,095 catches for 12,402 yards and 71 touchdowns and is a six-time Pro Bowler. Evans, who is in his 13th season, has 893 catches for 13,472 yards and 111 touchdowns and is a six-time Pro Bowler.

And Jalen Brunson did not take a single free throw.

Mike Brown reacts on the baseline during the fourth quarter of the Knicks' 115-107 loss to the Cavaliers on Nov. 11, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

I know at one time, the free throw advantage was 18-3. That's not why we lost the game, but you just hope to get a little bit better whistle when you're playing a good team like that, because it's already hard enough to go win a game. That was part of the reason why we lost the possession game. … James Harden had a great game as well. James shot as many free throws as our whole team."

It was the first time Brunson didn't take a single free throw since Feb. 11 last year.

#2761. Packers-Patriots on Fox leads Week 9 with 25.7 million

As NFL games go, the 4:25 p.m. ET window routinely has the biggest audience of the week — larger than any prime-time game.

That was true on Sunday, when 25.7 million on average tuned in for the window headlined by the Packers shredding the Patriots, 45-27.

It was the most-watched telecast of the week on any network, besting the Bills-Vikings game on Monday night by 4.0 million.

As the NFL is looks for more windows, and potentially heads toward more Wednesday night (and possibly Tuesday night) games, 4:25 p.m. ET on Sunday continues to be the best slot.

Currently, that window is shared by CBS and Fox, with one or the other having the game in most weeks. (A few times per year, they both have 4:25 p.m. ET games on the same day.) With all windows (except for Monday night, which is available a year later) opening up in 2030, it will be interesting to see whether the NFL decides to stop splitting all of Sunday afternoon between two networks and selling 4:25 p.m. ET as a weekly package.

Given the audiences generated by that window, it would easily generate the biggest rights fee of any of them.

#2760. Fantasy football start/sit advice for Week 10

Welcome to Week 10 of the fantasy football season! Below I have some NFL players and thoughts on their fantasy value this week. Remember, don't blindly bench guys who are "sits." Your fantasy team will dictate if you even can bench a player or not.

The Chicago Bears, Denver Broncos, Philadelphia Eagles, and Tampa Bay Buccaneers will take next week off for some rest.

We need to keep an eye on Lock's elbow and oblique injuries, but he's set up with a great matchup this week. The Raiders are second-worst in allowing fantasy points to quarterbacks. They've allowed two or more passing touchdowns in every game except the first two of the season, as Malik Willis and Justin Herbert only manage one touchdown a piece. I do expect the Seahawks to focus on running the ball, while the Raiders should have trouble offensively with rookie QB Fernando Mendoza taking the wheel against the Seahawks defense, which ranks Top 5 in most defensive categories this season.

We can't expect huge numbers from Lock, but when you've got Jaxon Smith-Njigba to throw to and a great matchup, points will be had.

Young had a good bounce back fantasy game last week against a top Broncos defense, as he completed 28-of-39 passes for 276 yards, three touchdowns and an interception. This week he gets a much better matchup against a Saints team that doesn't have a strong pass rush and have allowed the seventh-most fantasy points per game to QBs over the last five. Sheuder Sanders just threw for 297 yards and two touchdowns against them, while they rank in the bottom ten of almost all pass defense metrics so far.

Stafford gets a great matchup here, but he is now throwing to a lot of players not named Puka Nacua or Davante Adams. With Carson Beck starting for the Cardinals and Michael Wilson done for the year, the Rams are going to run the ball a ton. Start Kyren Williams and Blake Corum and be afraid of this passing offense without their best two receivers.

Can you sit Love at this point? A lot depends on your other choices, but playing the Vikings so far this season hasn't been profitable for opposing quarterbacks. Just last Monday night, Brian Flores' defense held the No. 1 fantasy QB, Josh Allen, to his worst fantasy finish of the season. Yes, Love put up the best game of the season against the Vikings back in Week 1, but I expect Flores now has plenty of film to scheme his defense to take on this great passing offense.

Charbonnet has finally returned to a big role in the Seahawks offense. Last week he out-touched Jadarian Price 17 to 13. Price ended up with a better PPR game due to his four receptions for 54 yards, but Charbonnet saw more snaps and routes run. This backfield is fairly evenly split, but I want Charbonnet, especially in games where the Seahawks should lead.

The Raiders have allowed the highest yards per rushing attempt and rushing yards per game to running backs this season. You can probably play both of the Seattle backs, but I would prioritize Charbonnet.

The Dolphins will likely trot Kyle McCord out at QB in Week 10 after Malik Willis went on IR with a groin injury. That's not great for the Dolphins offense, but the Colts just shipped off their two best defenders ahead of the trade deadline. Gordon had a tough matchup last week against a good Lions run defense, but that changes this week. The Colts rank seventh-worst at fantasy points allowed to running backs. They've also allowed the third-most rushing yards per game and fifth-most yards per carry at 4.9. I'd feel good about running Gordon out there in this matchup.

Can you really sit the white-hot Pollard this week? Maybe not, but I'd think about it in smaller leagues this week. The Jaguars run defense has been one of the best in the league so far. They're holding opposing running backs to 3.6 yards per carry and just five touchdowns through eight games. Of course Pollard has scored five touchdowns in his last two games, but we know he can't keep that up. Right?

I like Kiner as a desperation flex this week, as he should get the early down work with both Rhamondre Stevenson and TreVeyon Henderson out. But, if he was used more as a receiver, I'd feel much better about starting him this week. The Lions are much worse against the pass than the run and the Patriots are hurting at running back. I expect Drake Maye and company will throw it early and often in what could be a high scoring affair through the air. Unless the Patriots get out to a decent lead in Detroit, Kiner's usage is likely to be low in a pass-first matchup.

Coker has hit a lull in his season. After a hot start, he hasn't topped 36 receiving yards in any of his last three games. That has a lot to do with the Panthers offensive line and Bryce Young regressing. The good news is that Young played much better last week against a strong Broncos pass defense. That defense was able to hold down the Panthers receivers with Patrick Surtain and company, but Young took the path of least resistance by targeting their tight ends. This week the receivers get a much better matchup, as the Saints are bottom 10 in all pass rushing metrics and just gave up big games to the Browns two rookie receivers.

Tyson only saw around a quarter of the snaps last week, but he still managed to catch 4-of-5 targets. Yes, he fumbled on his first reception near the goal line, but it was a good play by the defender while Tyson tried to dive toward the end zone. I'm more worried about him tweaking his hamstring again than him fumbling.

Tyson's usage is just going to go up and when he's out there running routes, Tyler Shough is going to target him. Maybe we should wait another game, but the skill is there and it sure looks like the opportunity could be there this week.

We need to keep an eye on George Pickens' status for this week, but no matter if he plays or not, this is a tough matchup for the Cowboys wide receivers. The 49ers have allowed the fifth-fewest fantasy points to the position. They've also allowed the second-fewest yards per reception.

I do expect Pickens will return from his hamstring injury after almost playing last week. And if that's so, I feel even better about sitting Flournoy. He's had some nice games of late, but his usage remains low, with four or fewer targets in his last three games.

Tucker has been inconsistent this season, and now will get rookie Fernando Mendoza instead of Kirk Cousins throwing the ball toward him. I'd like to see how Mendoza looks before trusting Tucker. And against this Seahawks defense, I'm definitely sitting him. Seattle has given up the fewest fantasy points to opposing wide receivers. On the year, only four receivers have hit double-digit fantasy points against them.

The Colts just traded away Keenan Allen and Josh Downs and have none of their starting receivers left, which leaves Warren as the No. 1 receiver. Yes, the Dolphins' defense will focus on him, but the volume should outweigh extra defenders in his vicinity. You were probably going to start him anyway, but just let me give you an extra boost.

Washington has shown upside this season, but consistency remains tough in a run-first offense. This week though, the Steelers will be hard pressed to shut down the Bengals passing attack and should need to throw more often than they'd like. The good news is that the Bengals have been weak against tight ends this season, allowing the fifth-most receptions and the third most receiving yards against the position.

Can the Packers move the ball against the top defense in the league? Yes, they can. They did it in Week 1, but despite a 19-10 lead at halftime, the Vikings won the game 39-22. In that game, Carson Wentz threw three touchdowns in a come from behind victory, with the help of the defense holding the Packers to one field goal in the second half.

On the season, no tight end has hit double-digit fantasy points against the Vikings. Kraft did have three receptions for 65 yards in Week 1, which is the most yards the Vikings have allowed to a tight end all season. But, that's still not a good fantasy day and I don't want to bet on him being the first to hit over 9 fantasy points against the Vikings as a tight end.

The Rams currently have allowed the seventh-fewest fantasy points to tight ends this season. That's good, but doesn't sound like they are shutting down opposing tight ends completely. Well, that is true, but they've also faced George Kittle, Brock Bowers, Dalton Kincaid, McBride, and Isiah Likely. Those five tight ends combined to catch 21-of-34 targets for 178 yards and no touchdowns. That's 5.2 yards per target and 8.4 yards per catch. On the season they're allowing 5.8 yards per target and 9 yards per reception to tight ends. When the best tight ends come to play, the Rams show up. And with Michael Wilson done for the season, the team should be able to bracket McBride all game.

This week the Cardinals will start rookie Carson Beck. He should target McBride 10+ times while Aaron Donald and Myles Garrett harass him. Of course, this is one of, if not the top tight end in the league right now. You most-likely have to start him, but we should lower our expectations this week. And if you happen to have George Kittle going up against Dallas, I'd start George.

#2758. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2757. Bruins vs. Canadiens Rivalry, Explained: History, Iconic Moments and Why It Still Matters

Bruins vs. Canadiens Rivalry, Explained: History, Iconic Moments and Why It Still Matters originally appeared on NESN. Add NESN as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

One of the most famous rivalries in American sports is Yankees vs. Red Sox. In hockey, though, Bruins vs. Canadiens is the kind of matchup that can feel less like a regular-season game and more like a referendum on pride.

Boston and Montreal have been crossing paths for a century — first meeting in 1924 — and they've played each other more than any other pair of teams in NHL history. Through their most recent meeting listed (March 17, 2026), Montreal holds the overall edge in the series.

The best part (or worst part, depending on which sweater you wear): It's never just one era. Different stars, different rules, different leagues... same energy.

Here's a breakdown of the Bruins–Canadiens rivalry, from the beginning to what it looks like today.

The Bruins and Canadiens first played on Dec. 8, 1924, back when the NHL looked nothing like it does now. What turned it from "two teams who play against each other a lot" into a true blood feud was the frequency — and the stakes.

Once playoff meetings became more common, the rivalry got a postseason identity. The teams have met in the Stanley Cup Playoffs more times than any other matchup, and those series created the reputations that still hang in the air every time Boston and Montreal share the ice.

Some rivalries are fueled by geography. Some by championships. Bruins–Canadiens has both — plus a cultural edge and a history of playoff pain that's hard to replicate.

For long stretches, Montreal's dominance in postseason matchups gave the rivalry a specific bite. Then, Boston's own golden eras (and iconic defensemen) swung the storyline back the other way — so every generation gets its own version of the argument.

If you want the origin story of "this always gets nasty," start here. Montreal beat Boston 2–0 in the 1930 Stanley Cup Final. It's remembered as one of the earliest major chapters in the rivalry — and a reminder that, even then, these teams were rarely on neutral terms.

Sixteen years later, they met again in a best-of-seven Stanley Cup Final. Montreal won 4–1, and multiple games went to overtime, underlining how tight (and tense) these matchups could be even in the earliest eras.

This is the one Bruins fans still say with a sigh.

Game 7 of the 1979 semifinal swung on the infamous too-many-men penalty, followed by Guy Lafleur's late tying goal and Yvon Lambert's overtime winner. If you ever need a single example of why this rivalry lives forever, it's the way one whistle can become a scar.

After years of Montreal getting the better of Boston in the postseason, the Bruins finally flipped the script in 1988, winning the series 4–1 — their first playoff series win over the Canadiens since 1943, per contemporary reporting and historical tracking. For Boston, it wasn't just a series win — it felt like permission to breathe.

In 1992, Boston swept Montreal 4–0, a result that stood out precisely because sweeping the Canadiens in a best-of-seven was so rare. It was the kind of series Bruins fans still reference when they want to remind Montreal that "Yeah, it has happened."

Fast-forward to the modern era, and the rivalry delivered another classic. The Bruins dropped the first two games at home, clawed back and ultimately advanced in seven on Nathan Horton's Game 7 overtime winner. It was a perfect Bruins–Canadiens script — stressful, loud, and settled by one moment you never forget.

Even when the teams aren't meeting deep in the playoffs, they still find ways to turn a random night into a headline.

On Dec. 23, 2025, Montreal scored five straight goals — including four in a five-minute span — to pull away for a 6–2 win in a game described as "fight-filled."

The 2020s version may not have the same constant playoff collisions as earlier decades, but the matchups still carry weight — and they still get chippy fast when the game tilts.

A weird modern footnote: Boston and Montreal didn't play each other at all in the 2020–21 regular season because the NHL realigned divisions and limited schedules to intradivisional games during COVID-era travel restrictions. That alone tells you how rare the "no Bruins vs. Habs" concept is.

Since then, the rivalry has been kept fresh by new star power and the occasional game that boils over — like that December 2025 third-period avalanche — plus tight, high-drama games that still feel personal.

#2756. For Week 9, Next Gen Stats outpaced the McAfee alt cast again

The ESPN announcement of the viewership for a given Monday night game always requires a careful look for any information about the viewership of the multiple weekly alt casts on the Disney-owned networks.

This week, the release presented only the total viewership for all platforms, at 21.7 million. Nothing was said about the total or individual numbers for the McAfee alt cast on ESPN2 or the Next Gen Stats version on NFL Network.

Andrew Marchand of The Athletic has gotten the numbers. For the Next Gen Stats alt cast, 499,000 tuned in. For the McAfee ensemble, the number was 406,000.

The final tally gives Next Gen Stats a 2-0 lead over the McAfee option. The gap was more narrow this time around; when they first squared off for the Week 6 Commanders-49ers game, NFLN attracted 469,000 and ESPN2 got 351,000. (ESPN2 is in roughly 70 million homes; NFL Network’s distribution is at or below 50 million.)

The total viewership for that game was 17.9 million. With Bills-Vikings beating that figure by 21.2 percent, Next Gen Stats was up 6.3 percent over Week 6 and the McAfee cast increased by 15.6 percent.

Of the total audience for the game, 2.2 percent watched NFL Network and 1.87 percent watched ESPN2. Which raises, once again, a one-word question: Why?

The Monday night alt casts don’t draw in new viewers. It’s added expense, with no extra revenue for the broader operation. (Unless there’s a tangible benefit to siphoning viewers from ABC/ESPN for the other channels.)

Ultimately, it’s a zero-sum game. With alt casts, Bills-Vikings viewership was 21.7 million. Without alt casts, Bills-Vikings viewership undoubtedly would have been 21.7 million.

And that’s why the various ABC affiliates are upset that Disney will have a full slate of alt casts for Super Bowl LXI. The total number will be the total number, if the game is on one network or twenty. The alt casts will rob the Peter of ABC to pay the Paul of the various other platforms.

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2754. Value of Things: By the numbers

There are any number of ways to look at the effectiveness of an offense or defense. We have been alternating throughout the season on Thursdays to look at one unit or the other. Sometimes the numbers within the numbers reveal things that the overall numbers do not. For instance, when we look at the offense we see that it is succeeding in a number of ways. Obviously, we have looked at the lack of a run game over and over again, but when we look at the overall numbers we see something different.

Points per game: 26.1 (9th)Total Yards per game: 356.5 (11th)Yards per play: 5.8 (8th)Turnovers: 4 (1st)Sacks: 16 (10th)

So, essentially the Texans are a top ten offense when you look at scoring, total yards, turnovers, and sacks allowed. They are only really deficient in terms of running the ball. However, there are some cracks in the veneer and they came to the forefront when Nick Caley appeared before reporters for his press conference during the bye week. He was asked about tendencies and it became very obvious that he was not aware of them. Self scouting is always the most important scouting.

This seems natural in baseball which has always been my first love. One player hits .300 and the other hits .250. Obviously there are numerous other numbers to come to the forefront, but in general you play the more effective player. This can be seen when we look at the pass catchers on the Texans. We can look at just catches, yards, and touchdowns, but we can also look at snaps, targets, catch percentage, yards per target, and success rate. In other words, who is getting the most playing time and who should be getting the most playing time. We will look at snaps on passing plays only since we are including tight ends and running backs.

No single number is the holy grail here. All of these numbers work together in concert. For instance, Tank Dell obviously has a lower catch percentage and success rate but he also has the highest yards per target. Obviously, he is valuable as a down the field threat and should probably be on the field as much as possible even given his low catch percentage and success rate. Those measurements don't account for how much bang the Texans get for their buck and it doesn't account for how his presence impacts the other receivers.

However, there is something glaring that many people have noticed outside of Kirby. Xavier Hutchinson leads the wide receivers in passing down snaps and targets. So, obviously Hutchinson should be the best receiver on the Texans. Unfortunately, the numbers don't bare that out. He is fourth among wide receivers in catch percentage, fourth in yards per target, and fourth in success rate. I might be nuts, but I'm not seeing exactly what Hutchinson brings to the table.

This is the difference between looking at basic numbers and looking at the more advanced numbers. He is on pace to have 51 catches and over 600 yards receiving. He could have four or five touchdown catches at that pace. That feels like a pretty good season for a guy that is a second or third wide receiver. He is likely to be second on the team in catches at that pace. So, of course he is a solid wide receiver right?

Except Boutte is a better down the field threat as well and Marvin Harrison Jr. looks like he could be a better possession receiver once he gets acclimated. Not only that, but Hutchinson was getting more snaps than Jaylin Noel who had very similar numbers to Tank Dell before he went down. Obviously there is more to football than just numbers, but it seems logical that you would want the players on the field that are going to be the most successful the most often.

That has never been Xavier Hutchinson. Even when you go back to the last game you go back to Hutchinson leading the wide receiver group in snaps. That just doesn't make a whole lot of sense. We realize that Harrison is still adjusting, but Boutte has been here all season and he clearly is more effective. Moving forward you could justify Harrison as WR5 given the numbers, but you could also surmise that he will get better as he gets acclimated to the offense.

Moving forward Hutchinson should really be WR4 at the very best. Obviously, Dalton Schultz should be on the field as much as possible too as an underneath or intermediate target if you are going to go with Boutte and Dell as the other two wide receivers. A part of the art of running the most efficient offense possible is putting the best eleven guys on the field as much as possible. That means knowing what you have been doing and whether it has worked or not.

#2753. Sean McVay laughs as he says he's tempted to ask assistant coach Robert Woods to suit up

He didn’t sound completely serious, but Rams coach Sean McVay said on Wednesday that he talked to assistant wide receivers coach Robert Woods about whether he could suit up and play while receivers Puka Nacua, Davante Adams and Jordan Whittington are all out with injuries.

McVay was asked during his press conference whether he has talked to the 34-year-old Woods, who was a receiver for the Rams for five years, about potentially playing again.

“I have, yes,” McVay answered with a laugh.

McVay acknowledged that there are Collective Bargaining Agreement rules against a coach becoming a player, but he said Woods is still in great shape and it would be fun to give it a try.

“I’ve looked at him, and the way he looks out there, I’ve definitely been tempted,” McVay said. “I think that’d be a violation of the CBA. I don’t know what he’d have to do, but he looks damn good and still is smooth out there. Very tempting.”

Woods was cut by the Steelers before the 2025 season and hasn’t played since, and he announced his retirement and signed a one-day contract with the Rams in February of 2026. Realistically, even with his intimate knowledge of the Rams’ offense, he probably wouldn’t be able to contribute as much on the field as whatever receiver the Rams could add off waivers or another team’s practice squad.

If Woods and the Rams actually wanted him to become an active player, it wouldn’t be as simple as signing him to the active roster, as the league has rules preventing that. Players on the reserve-retired list have to pass through waivers before they can join a team. And the NFL generally doesn’t allow coaches to become players: In 2020, when the Broncos lost all their quarterbacks to covid, they asked the league office if they could activate one of their assistant coaches and were told no. In 2009, Eagles coach Andy Reid wanted to play coaching intern Matt Nagy at quarterback in a preseason game and was also told by the league that he couldn’t.

So the idea of Woods playing receiver for the Rams is probably not going to get any kind of serious consideration. Even if, with all the Rams’ injuries, it’s tempting.

#2752. Taylor Fritz loves the rule change being tested at the Stockholm Open this week, ‘it definitely helps’

The American star, a former US Open finalist, defeated Norrie 7-6, 6-3 in the Swedish capital.

Fritz was made to work hard for his victory, saving one set point in the first set and multiple break points at 3-3 in the second set.

The American ultimately prevailed and can now look forward to a clash with Holger Rune in the next round.

Following Wednesday's contest, Fritz was asked about a potential rule change being tested in Stockholm this week: fresh balls are being introduced every seven games, rather than the usual nine.

It would be fair to say that Fritz is happy with the change.

"I think the most effective solution [for the quality of balls] is fixing the s–– balls, but this new rule definitely helps. Like, it definitely helps," Fritz told the Tennis Master in Stockholm.

"This would have been a very, very, miserable match to play if we were not switching after seven. Because even with the seven and seven switch, the first ball change for me was brutal because of the warm-up, actually.

"You wouldn't think it, but just adding that extra warm up plus the first seven games, at the end of the first ball change, it was already like, 'Wow, I need new balls. This is getting really rough.' It's tough to play.

"So after we got past that, changing on every seven was huge, honestly. The thing is, it matters so much on the surface, and it's really tough to tell, because here the court is tearing the balls up much more than what was happening in Paris and what was happening in Basel.

"So, the balls just react differently to different courts and different weather when we're playing outside.

"So I said this before, some weeks seven and nine is actually okay. Most weeks, seven and seven is probably better, but it'll change week to week, and this is definitely one of those weeks where a quicker ball change is needed for sure."

Fritz has long been a vocal proponent for improving the quality of tennis balls used on the ATP Tour.

Fritz recently complained about the balls used at the Shanghai Masters, stating that they 'wear out so fast.'

Fritz is far from the only individual to have voiced concerns regarding the quality of tennis balls used on the Tour in recent years.

At the Rotterdam Open in February, Daniil Medvedev described the HEAD Tour XT balls as 'terrible'.

Fritz and Rune – who recently returned from a long-term injury suffered at the 2025 Stockholm Open – first played each other at the 2023 Miami Open.

Fritz came out on top against the 2022 Paris Masters champion, triumphing 6-3, 6-4 in the Round of 16.

Just under one year later, Rune avenged his Miami defeat.

The Danish youngster beat Fritz 2-6, 7-6, 6-3 in the 2024 Indian Wells Round of 16. Rune went on to lose in the quarter-finals to Daniil Medvedev.

Fritz bounced back emphatically in their third and final contest, beating Rune 6-1, 6-2 in the 2024 Shanghai Masters Round of 16.

Fritz defeated David Goffin in the quarter-finals, before losing to Novak Djokovic in the last four.

#2750. LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome

LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James' latest week with the Sixers included a sip of a stranger's wine, yet another number no NBA player had reached and a return to the city where his career began.

It also included his worst performance with Philadelphia, followed less than 48 hours later by his highest-scoring game with the team.

Welcome to the fourth edition of the LeBron Log.

No chronicle of James' first season in Philadelphia could omit the time he helped himself to a fan's wine during a game.

James chased Tyrese Maxey's transition pass into the stands during the third quarter of the Sixers' Nov. 2 win over the Bulls. He collided with Philadelphia native Troy Clemons, spilled some of Clemons' wine and sampled what had landed on his arm.

The interaction received its own story on our site, and deservedly so. It also belongs in the Log.

Clemons did not keep the cup as a souvenir. He threw it away and got another drink.

James returned to the court and later broke into a dance after scoring over the 6-foot-1 Tre Jones. The wine clip generated millions of views across social media and became the defining image from an otherwise ordinary 19-point Sixers victory.

The following night in Detroit, James offered a broader explanation for why Philadelphia has already looked comfortable together.

"We've got a good team," James said. "Guys vibe with one another. Guys like each other. Guys enjoy being around each other. That has a lot to do with it, too. It makes the game a lot easier when you enjoy being around people."

Eleven games into his Sixers career, James appears to be doing exactly that.

James arrived in Detroit two baskets away from another unprecedented number.

His second field goal against the Pistons made him the first player in NBA history to reach 16,000 regular-season field goals.

James finished the Sixers' 112-106 victory with 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. The near-triple-double helped Philadelphia improve to 8-1 and avenge its only loss of the season.

Kareem Abdul-Jabbar ranks second in league history with 15,837 made field goals. Karl Malone is a distant third at 13,528. James is the only active player with more than 12,000.

His scoring record has long been secure. James passed Abdul-Jabbar in career points in February 2023 and later overtook him in made baskets. The round number in Detroit still illustrated the distance he has created between himself and every other scorer.

James began his career by making 5,361 field goals across seven seasons with Cleveland. He added 3,510 in Miami, returned to make 3,691 more for the Cavaliers and made another 3,421 during eight years with the Lakers.

Basket No. 16,000 belonged to Philadelphia.

The Sixers' 8-1 start also carried a notable James connection. Each of his previous four teams to begin a season at least 7-1 reached the NBA Finals.

James was not ready to entertain that conversation in early November.

"It's too early to be talking about the Finals and things of that nature," he said. "We've got to continue to buy into what we want to do."

Two nights later, James returned to the place where nearly half of those 16,000 baskets were made.

His first game in Cleveland as a Sixer brought out jerseys from every stop of his career. Cavaliers colors filled Rocket Arena alongside Heat black, Lakers purple and the Sixers' throwback look.

James received a warm ovation when he was introduced last among Philadelphia's starters. When he checked out late in the fourth quarter, with the result decided, the crowd stood for him again.

James has experienced both ends of Cleveland's emotional range.

He returned as a member of the Heat on Dec. 2, 2010, to one of the most hostile receptions in NBA history. Six years later, he delivered the Cavaliers their first championship and ended Cleveland's 52-year major professional sports title drought.

"I've seen the depths of hell when it comes to a reception here, all the way to the heavens above," James said. "Anything in between is easy money."

The reception fell somewhere in the middle. His performance landed much closer to the bottom.

James finished with eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, three assists and six turnovers in the Sixers' 119-95 loss. It was his first single-digit performance in Cleveland since he scored three points against the Rockets on Dec. 30, 2004.

He had scored at least 10 points in 396 consecutive regular-season games in the city. The run covered his first stint with the Cavaliers, every visit as an opponent and his entire second tenure with the organization.

It ended during his first trip back in a Sixers uniform.

"I definitely did not feel in a good rhythm offensively," James said. "A couple of them were careless. A couple of them were just trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

It was only the 10th single-digit regular-season game of James' 1,630-game career. He still took time afterward to consider what an eventual jersey retirement in Cleveland could represent.

"The journey from being an 18-year-old rookie, to leading a franchise to a championship," James said. "And everything in between."

James did not carry the rough night into Chicago.

Less than 48 hours after struggling in Cleveland, he scored a season-high 22 points in the Sixers' 127-121 victory over the Bulls. Thirteen came during the second quarter, when James helped Philadelphia turn an early deficit into a halftime lead.

His first basket produced another milestone.

James entered Sunday trailing Abdul-Jabbar's 1,895 points for the most scored against the Bulls in NBA history. His first-quarter three-pointer moved him into the lead. He ended the afternoon with 1,916.

The most interesting play from his night was a pass.

With the Sixers ahead by four and 2:20 remaining, James drove into the paint. Three Chicago defenders collapsed toward him. Maxey stood between the left wing and corner, but even he did not believe James could see him.

James fired the ball through the defense. Maxey sidestepped Isaac Okoro and made the three-pointer, pushing Philadelphia's lead to seven.

After his postgame interview, James pulled out his phone and watched the play again.

"He didn't think I saw him," James called out from his locker as reporters asked Maxey about the possession.

Maxey acknowledged that he had positioned himself to rebound because he expected James to shoot.

"Honestly, I was like this," Maxey said, raising his hands. "I thought he was about to shoot a layup."

James has spent the opening weeks of the season teaching his new teammates to expect the unexpected pass.

"You've got to become a wide receiver or a catcher at that point," Maxey said. "You never know when you're going to get the ball.

#2748. Dolphins QB Kyle McCord: Experience in big games something I can draw from

The Dolphins placed Malik Willis on injured reserve this week and that means Kyle McCord will be making his first NFL start when they face the Colts on Sunday.

It will also be McCord’s first regular season action and first game snaps of any kind with the Dolphins. The 2025 sixth-round pick spent last season on the Eagles’ practice squad and spent the summer with the Packers before being traded to Miami at the end of August.

That’s a lot of firsts for one game, but McCord said his time at Ohio State and Syracuse is “something that you definitely can hang your hat on” because it gave him experience on big stages.

“It’s a blessing, but I think once you get the first play call on the helmet and get going, I think it’ll feel just like football,” McCord said, via a transcript from the team. “And I feel like going back to what I was just saying, that’s something that having played in those big college games, I think you’ve been in those moments before. It’s not my first time I’m playing in a big game. So yeah, I feel like that’s where I’m drawing a lot of confidence from.”

McCord would not be doing himself any favors by making more of the moment than it is, but that first play call and first snap against NFL opposition will likely lead to a quick realization that it’s a different world from his last time in the saddle for a meaningful game.

#2747. Ravens not planning change to starting offensive line after Ryan Neuzil trade

The Ravens made a trade for center Ryan Neuzil this week, but they aren’t planning on throwing him right into the lineup in Week 10.

Neuzil started every game for the Falcons this season and he also made 17 starts in 2025. The Falcons’ offensive line coach in 2025 was current Ravens offensive line coach Dwayne Ledford, so Neuzil feels he understands “what [Ledford] wants and what he sees in the linemen.”

That knowledge should help Neuzil pick things up on the fly, but head coach Jesse Minter said Neuzil’s arrival doesn’t mean current center Cam Jurgens will be knocked out of the lineup.

“I think he brings versatility,” Minter said, via the team’s website. “I don’t anticipate anything changing with Cam and what position he plays. We think he’s like the future of our offensive line. I think it’s just a piece that adds depth. I do think he’ll have flexibility as he gets here, as he gets going. Wherever that leads I think we’ll see as we go.”

The Ravens have had to change plans at center multiple times since Tyler Linderbaum’s departure during the offseason. If they need to make another one, Neuzil will give them an experienced option to plug the hole.

#2746. Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36

Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Round 1 of the new-look Sixers vs. the new-look Celtics decisively went Boston's way on Tuesday night.

In the first meeting between the teams since the July trade that sent Jaylen Brown to Philadelphia and Paul George to Boston, the Celtics earned a 123-101 win at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

The Sixers dropped to 9-3 and the Celtics improved to 9-2.

George was the game's brightest star, scoring 36 points on 14-for-22 shooting. Jayson Tatum added 21 points.

Tyrese Maxey's 24 points led the Sixers. Brown was hampered by foul trouble in his heavily anticipated first outing against Tatum. He posted 13 points, five rebounds and two assists in 26 minutes.

The Sixers will host the Raptors on Thursday night. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Celtics:

To no one's surprise, Brown and Tatum guarded each other to open the night.

VJ Edgecombe defended a former teammate in George, who scored Boston's first four points with a pair of pure jumpers. On the other end, Maxey made the Sixers' first three field goals.

Judging by volume, the home fans cared most about the much-scrutinized Brown vs. Tatum matchup. The crowd was excited early when Brown snagged a steal and then dished to Maxey for a fast-break three-pointer. The fans also dialed up the noise when Brown sized up Tatum in isolation. He wound up dribbling the ball off his foot and out of bounds. Tatum pointed to signal it was Celtics ball.

Boston's star forward had a productive first quarter, posting 11 points. He scored an easy two when he used a back screen to break free from Brown and convert a layup that put the Celtics up 11-7. Boston began 9 for 12 from the floor as a team.

As for Brown, he sat shortly after being called for his second foul with 6:48 left in the first quarter.

Brown re-entered at the start of the second quarter. The Sixers still struggled to eat into Boston's lead.

A Sam Hauser triple extended the Celtics' advantage to 12 points. There was some audible frustration in the building after Brown missed two free throws on the next possesion.

The Sixers' deficit hit 16 points on a Hugo Gonzalez jumper. Meanwhile, the Sixers missed 12 of their first 14 three-point tries. While some of those shots were open looks that simply didn't go down, the Sixers also rushed up several contested jumpers before they made Boston's defense do serious work.

In the bigger picture, it wouldn't be remotely surprising if shooting stands out as a weak area for the Sixers come the trade deadline. They entered the game ranked 27th in the NBA in three-point frequency outside of garbage time and 22nd in three-point accuracy, according to Cleaning the Glass.

Joel Embiid (16 points, six rebounds) had an injury scare late in the second quarter when he fell hard after missing a layup in transition. He rose gingerly and limped through the final 30 seconds or so of the first half.

The Sixers' star big man was fine to start the third quarter and even drained a long-overdue three on the opening possession. He'd been in a terrible outside shooting slump (3 for 32 from deep on the season) before that shot.

Brown's foul trouble persisted. He picked up his fourth personal with 10:40 to go in the third quarter.

The Sixers stormed back without him and their defense played a large role. After LeBron James rumbled in for a layup, Dean Wade stonewalled Payton Pritchard on the perimeter to force a shot-clock violation. Wade later canned a three to even the contest at 76-all.

The Celtics responded and Tatum led the charge. His third-quarter exploits included a highlight slam on James and a top-of-the-key three to build Boston's lead back up to nine points.

Pritchard buried a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter and the Celtics' advantage kept growing. George celebrated a triple that stretched his team's lead to 100-82. The Sixers' spirits were considerably lower as they headed to their bench for a timeout. As a Sixer, George only had one higher-scoring game (39 points) than his performance Tuesday.

George and Tatum were each excellent in Philadelphia. Outside of Maxey, who finished 8 for 12 from the field, the Sixers didn't receive similar games from their stars.

Brown will be back in Boston on Jan. 21 when the Sixers visit the Celtics. He'll try to even the score.

#2745. George Russell takes major step outside F1 with ambitious new project

George Russell has announced the launch of his own karting academy, an initiative designed to identify promising young drivers and help them overcome the financial barriers that often prevent talented youngsters from progressing in motorsport.

The Mercedes driver unveiled the GR63 Academy on Thursday, 12 November, through his official Instagram account. The programme will be run in partnership with Silverstone and the British Racing Drivers' Club (BRDC), with 25 children set to receive places in its inaugural season in 2027.

Russell hopes the initiative will give aspiring racing drivers an opportunity to develop their abilities, particularly those who might otherwise struggle to secure the financial support needed to pursue their ambitions.

Announcing the project on social media, Russell reflected on the importance of karting in his own journey towards Formula 1 and explained why he wants to help the next generation of drivers.

"So proud to introduce you all to the GR63 Academy. Karting is where it all started for me and has played a crucial role in my career," the Briton wrote.

"I also know how hard it is for talented young drivers to get that first opportunity, especially when budgets are tight."

Russell made clear that his ambition goes beyond simply identifying young drivers with potential. He also wants to ensure they receive the assistance and guidance necessary to take the next steps in their careers.

"With the GR63 Academy, I want to give something back to my sport by finding those hidden stars and giving them the support and guidance they need to reach their potential."

The selection process will take place at Silverstone, beginning with qualifying sessions in December 2026. The initial stage will cost £25 to enter, with the most promising participants progressing to a further selection round in January 2027.

The academy will ultimately select 25 young drivers to compete in a fully funded eight-round championship between March and November 2027.

At the end of the season, the five leading competitors will progress to a final assessment, with the successful driver earning a fully funded karting campaign in 2028.

Russell also confirmed his involvement in selecting the participants, explaining how the first stages of the programme will determine who earns a place: "Following the first two rounds at Silverstone, I'll identify 25 kids to take part in the GR63 Academy in 2027."
